#137. Is bad news for Jensen Huang also bad news for the AI industry?

We didn’t see this coming. Or at least, I didn’t — a week ago, when I wrote about Nvidia’s board opening an investigation into then-CEO Jensen Huang, I assumed his job was safe.

On the latest episode of TechCrunch’s Equity podcast, Rebecca Bellan, Tim De Chant, and I discussed how Huang’s ouster went down and what it means for the larger AI economy. While Tim said he saw it coming (albeit not this quickly), Rebecca said, “I didn’t think it was going to happen at all.”

“Jensen is Nvidia, right?” she added. “And so much of this economy is riding on Nvidia.”

Similarly, I marveled at how quickly Huang’s support seemed to crumble, with more scandals seeming to emerge every day. (At the same time, there are many who’d still like to see him return.) And we also took a moment to wonder what will happen to Nvidia’s investments in the larger AI ecosystem.

Keep reading for a preview of our conversation, edited for length and clarity; you can listen to the whole episode here.

Tim De Chant: I figured this was coming. I didn’t think it would happen this quickly. I thought there would be at least a few days.

Rebecca Bellan: I didn’t think it was going to happen at all. I mean, Jensen is Nvidia, right? And so much of this economy is riding on Nvidia. And we’ve got nothing but the last few years of data to show us that billionaires can do whatever the hell they want. So it was surprising to me  — it almost restored my faith in humanity, that he resigned.

But someone said to me, “If this were Elon, nothing would happen.” He would not be resigning. Nothing would be going down

Anthony Ha: Which is interesting, because I would have said, as of a week ago, or even just a few days ago, that Jensen is in the same category as Elon. I mean, no one is quite as special as Elon is, but it’s a big, incredibly successful company. Like you said — in a lot of ways, the entire AI economy is built on top of Nvidia.

And for most people, Jensen Huang is Nvidia. He’s the biggest shareholder. He has family members who work at the company, and probably most importantly, there was no succession plan in place. So this was not something that they were prepared for at all.

Rebecca: I think we should actually take a step back, because we just launched into this conversation assuming — rightly so — that our listeners will know what the heck we’re talking about.

But for those who somehow don’t, Jensen resigned on Thursday, after it came out he was having an affair with a former executive at an Nvidia customer called Megaspeed, who’s alleged to have ties to Chinese intelligence. And then there was another reported affair with an Nvidia executive.

So he’s resigned. Nvidia CFO Collette Kress is jumping in as the interim CEO. She’s a longtime board member. I think it’s always interesting when a CFO steps in to be a CEO. It just means we’re going to manage expenses right now, like that’s the key.

Anthony: I think a big part of the message is: This is not necessarily someone you should expect to be in this role for the long term. No disrespect to her, but it feels like a caretaker appointment while they figure things out.

Just to get into that backstory a little bit more, what has been really remarkable to me has been this sense that a lot of what ultimately brought Huang down, [that] stuff was at least known in intelligence circles for a while. Apparently, this report that [raises] questions, concerns about his relationship with Alice Huang is something that was circulating a year ago, or close to a year ago.

And then suddenly, everything really accelerated, Starting over the weekend, with Jensen Huang admitting to having an affair, the board saying it was investigating, but that it still had a lot of faith in him. And then this week, this other affair being reported on, and it just felt like there was all this bad news coming fairly quickly.

But also, it wasn’t this thing that no one had known before. I guess a lot of the general public hadn’t known. So it’s this combined feeling of, “Wow, the ground kind of shifted so quickly under him,” even though the worst stuff, the possibility that somehow national security might have been threatened, is something that actually has been known by the government for nearly a year, if not more.

Rebecca: That’s exactly right. Trump was given some warnings about Huang’s affair with someone who has ties to Chinese intelligence, potentially, and it seemed like he didn’t really mind.

I think what’s important here, though, is to note that Jensen Huang has been an advocate for loosening chip exports to China. And so this is where the crux of it is. Can anyone trust him as the CEO of this company when he’s advocating for loosening chip exports to China? Which a lot of people believe is a national security issue, because that means it will give Chinese AI companies access to our powerful chips, which will help them improve their AI, which could threaten America.

I mean, there’s two sides of this coin. And I think, honestly, that Jensen does not need a woman to tell him that  — he just wants to sell more chips, and to anybody and everybody, and whether or not that’s good for national security or not, I think is honestly secondary to him.

Tim:  I completely agree. I think the big turning point seems to happen on November 9 — so that would have been earlier this week, Monday, when the Bloomberg report came out and suggested that Nvidia’s head attorney had some role in inquiring about the Department of Commerce’s investigation into Alice Huang.

Things seem to have accelerated in the last few days following that. And I don’t know what role exactly that report played in it, but it seems to be maybe there was a shift within government or within the board, a kind of a daunting realization of the extent to which this had spread just beyond Jensen and some affair.

Anthony: There’s also just the question of: What does this mean for Nvidia? What does this mean for the broader AI industry? And one of the reasons those two things are very tightly connected is not just Nvidia chips themselves, but also the fact that under Jensen Huang, the company has invested very, very heavily in a number of AI startups.

People talk about the idea of an AI circular economy, and I think Nvidia has been one of the biggest players in that, in terms of both funding a lot of things, [but] also, obviously, providing the infrastructure for a lot of AI projects. So if Nvidia is going into this stage of lockdown, circle the wagons — even if it just means a small pullback, that could have really dramatic repercussions.

Rebecca: Yeah, that’s what I want to know: What happens to the Bank of Nvidia without Jensen? Is he the one leading this?

Just to throw some numbers at you, Nvidia apparently accounts for roughly 80% of the AI GPU market. It reported $99 billion in investments and another $25 billion in commitments, plus $36 billion dollars in cloud service commitments. A lot of those include investments into CoreWeave, Nebius, OpenAI, Anthropic, major data center projects. They’ve got their fingers in literally everybody’s pie.

#135. Two Celebrity Cases Put a Spotlight on British Courts’ Gag Orders

Russell Brand and the TV personality David Walliams both persuaded courts to suppress misconduct allegations against them. Some say the libel laws need to change.

Two recent cases have brought new attention to the ways that British laws can allow the wealthy and powerful to silence their critics.

The first was the rape and sexual assault trial of the comedian Russell Brand. Before his conviction last week, Mr. Brand had obtained an injunction prohibiting another accuser from telling almost anyone — including the police, her family and most doctors — about her claims that he assaulted her. (Mr. Brand has denied her allegations.) That injunction, first revealed by Dan Neidle, a tax lawyer and free-expression activist, apparently remains in force.

In the second instance, two British news organizations were temporarily blocked from reporting allegations of sexual misconduct against David Walliams, a television personality and author of children’s books, before a judge declined to make the injunction permanent. The Guardian newspaper and ITV News, a British broadcaster, published investigations last week into the allegations, which Mr. Walliams denies.

Though both men now face public scrutiny, the revelations of how they used court injunctions — essentially gag orders — to protect their reputations have provoked no shortage of public and political outrage. Judges in other cases have issued “super injunctions,” which prohibit publishing any information even about the injunction itself.

“What will the government do to prevent these abuses of process, and stop the abusive injunctions and super injunctions which stop scrutiny, accountability and the work of the free press?” Nick Timothy, the shadow justice minister, asked in Parliament on Tuesday.

The British government is now “looking at what we can do,” Alex Davies-Jones, the government minister responsible for victims of crime and violence against women and girls, said in response.

#133. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#132. Chris Van Hollen says he has path to win Senate leader over Chuck Schumer

Progressive US senator calling for more inclusion within Democratic caucus cites openness to change among peers

Democratic US senator Chris Van Hollen on Sunday escalated his bid to lead his party’s incoming Senate majority rather than Chuck Schumer, asserting that he has a path to victory amid a dedicated campaign that includes a series of television appearances.

Van Hollen, a Maryland progressive, appeared on Sunday on CNN, ABC and Fox News as he continues to make his case for why his peers in the Senate should reject Schumer, who has led the Democratic caucus for nearly a decade.

On CNN’s State of the Union, he reaffirmed that he believes he has a path to victory, citing support and a widespread openness to change within the Senate Democratic caucus.

“This really is a response to the call of the voters, who clearly rejected Donald Trump and his awful policies, but they haven’t fully hired the Democrats,” Van Hollen said, alluding to how the US president’s Republican party lost control of Congress during the 3 November midterm elections.

“They’ve given us a chance, and I think we need to earn their trust. And I think that begins by not going back to business as usual.”

Van Hollen criticized reports that Schumer’s team may move the Senate caucus leadership election date earlier than 1 December. He contended that the caucus should have a debate about the group’s direction as the Democrats assume control of Congress in January for the second half of Trump’s second presidency.

Van Hollen also responded to a list that was reportedly leaked by Schumer’s team to the political news outlet the Hill that revealed the committed votes the New York senator claims to already have. Van Hollen said that some of those senators on the list have actually already pledged to vote for him instead of Schumer.

He also called for more “inclusion and democracy” within the Democratic caucus, accusing Schumer of trying to exclude the more progressive members of the party. Van Hollen alluded to victories by Democratic senators-elect whose candidacies Schumer did not support – Abdul El-Sayed of Michigan and Troy Jackson of Maine – and said they “would not be there” if Schumer had his way.

“He joined with Aipac and crypto to try to defeat them,” Van Hollen said, referring in part to the American Israel Public Affairs Committee, which opposed El-Sayed and Jackson.

Appearing on ABC News’s This Week, Van Hollen accused Schumer’s team of putting out misinformation by leaking the alleged list of supporters, equating it to an intimidation tactic.

“They’re essentially trying to instill fear in people who may say that they’re going to support me as a new leader,” he said. “We just had a great day for our democracy around the country. How about a little democracy in the Senate Democratic caucus?”

On Fox News Sunday, Van Hollen responded more directly to the Hill’s report, calling it “a bunch of nonsense”. The outlet reported being told by a Schumer confidant that Van Hollen has “no chance of winning” – and that progressives are only using his candidacy to gain leverage over Schumer to sign on to a “liberal wishlist of priorities”.

“This is a wide open race,” Van Hollen said. “The other team has been putting out numbers that are flatly wrong.

“They’re trying to create this false sense of momentum. If they really believed that they were ahead, they wouldn’t be trying to move up the elections.”

Van Hollen also said that the number of senator supporters he had as of Sunday was “15 and growing”.

“I agree with the American people and all our candidates who ran across the country that this is not about left versus right. It’s not about progressives or moderates or conservatives,” he said. “Those are old Washington labels. It’s much more about taking on a rigged economy, rigged in favor of the very wealthy and fueled by political corruption.”

The race will be the first contested election for Democratic leader in decades. The closed-door, secret-ballot election is slated for early December.

Schumer, the leader of the Democratic minority in the current Senate, had immediately responded to Van Hollen’s initial challenge for leadership in a social media post on Thursday, writing that he welcomed “any discussion about the future of our caucus and the important work ahead”. But he added that he had “the votes to continue serving as Democratic leader”.

The Democratic caucus has privately deliberated about what message to take from the midterm elections – whether they truly represents an upswing in popularity for the party or if it was simply a firm rejection of Trump’s far-right policies.

Several of the incoming Democratic senators have voiced their desire for new leadership.

Beside El-Sayed and Jackson, that includes Senators-elect Adam Hamilton of Kansas, Annie Andrews of South Carolina, Chris Pappas of New Hampshire, and Peggy Flanagan of Minnesota.

Bernie Sanders, the progressive senator from Vermont, also recently said that he would not support Schumer’s bid to serve as the Democratic leader.

Meanwhile, Scott Colom, the Democratic senator-elect who recently achieved a historic upset in Mississippi, said he was in “listening mode” when it came to who he would cast his vote for to lead his party’s Senate caucus.

“I’m going to figure out what are the things that people are doing well in leadership, what are the things they’re not doing well, and find out how we can work together to achieve results,” Colom said Sunday on NBC News’s Meet the Press.

Schumer did help recruit many of the party’s winning candidates and raised money for them.

But many say Democratic Senate candidates Angie Nixon in Florida and Jamie Davis in Louisiana could have won races they narrowly lost in their states had they been given more support from the party establishment, including Schumer.

#130. Senator-elect Scott Colom vows to ensure Trump follows through on his promises

Mississippi Democrat points to commitments Trump made: lower prices, halt endless wars and release Epstein files

After recently becoming the first Mississippi Democrat elected to the US Senate in more than four decades, Scott Colom pledged to try to ensure Donald Trump “follows through on the commitments” he made while successfully running for a second presidency in 2024.

Colom, while being interviewed by CNN host Dana Bash on Sunday, alluded to various campaign promises made by Trump – including lowering consumer prices, halting the seemingly endless wars waged under other presidential administrations, and fully releasing files related to the prosecution of Jeffrey Epstein, the late accused sex trafficker whose social circle encompassed the US and global elite.

But prices for fuel and other goods soared as Trump and Israel started war in Iran in February, a conflict which has dragged on for months – and critics have said many of the so-called Epstein files have been excluded from allegedly incomplete disclosures by the president’s administration.

Voters “elected me to make sure he follows through on the commitments he made in 2024,” Colom said. “Well, I want to work … to actually accomplish that.”

Colom also said that he intended to support efforts to pass legislation restricting stock trading by members of Congress, saying it was important for Americans to know lawmakers at the US Capitol in Washington DC “aren’t making money instead of looking out for them”.

“We [have] got to look out for our families – not DC families,” Colom said.

In a separate interview with NBC News’s Kristen Welker on Sunday, Colom added: “I’m not here to look out for the billionaires. I’m here to look out for working people.”

Those remarks from Colom came after he unseated Republican incumbent Cindy Hyde-Smith during the 3 November midterm elections to become the first Black US senator from Mississippi since 1875 – during the Reconstruction period after the American civil war.

Colom’s triumph over Hyde-Smith occurred about three years after she blocked the former state prosecutor’s nomination for a federal judgeship during Joe Biden’s presidency, which interrupted Trump’s two White House terms. In many ways Colom’s victory punctuated a midterm “blue wave” that saw Trump and his Republican party lose control of Congress to their Democratic counterparts for the latter half of his second presidency beginning in January.

With the outcome of the US Senate race in Alaska still pending on Sunday, the seat to which Colom was elected was among eight that the Democrats had flipped in that congressional chamber. Democrats had also gained more than three dozen seats in the US House during the midterms while significantly expanding their control of state governors’ offices, igniting hopes among the party’s supporters that it could impede Trump’s agenda for the rest of his time in power.

Trump has responded to the new political reality for the rest of his presidency by reminding the public that he has veto powers which he can wield to dictate policy with respect to matters such as the Iran war.

Asked by Bash to reflect on the historic significance of his win over Hyde-Smith, Colom replied: “It says that we have made a lot of progress in the journey to have people judged by the content of their character and not the color of their skin.

“I had faith that Mississippians would give me a chance … if I could get my values out there and a vision for our state out there,” Colom said. “And – you know – Mississippi proved me right.”

Colom appeared on both networks remotely from Atlanta, where he was stranded after his flight to Washington DC was cancelled in the chaotic aftermath of Friday’s nationwide Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) ground stop.

From Atlanta’s airport on Saturday night, Colom documented the frustrations of fellow travelers on social media.

The senator-elect said one traveler even made a reference to his viral campaign ad criticizing Hyde-Smith for once suggesting those concerned about rising beef prices could opt for cheaper sources of protein.

The campaign ad’s tagline was: “Pick another protein? … I’d rather pick another senator instead.”

As the clock passed midnight at the airport, Colom wrote that another passenger made a joke at the expense of Trump’s transportation secretary Sean Duffy, saying, “Pick another flight? … pick another transportation secretary.”

#129. Texas puts to death man who beheaded three children, hours after Oklahoma execution

Oklahoma man, 73, executed for killing ex’s estranged husband, as US nears 40 executions for second straight year

A Texas man convicted of fatally stabbing and beheading his three young children in a squalid, windowless apartment in 2003 was recently executed.

John Allen Rubio, 46, was pronounced dead at 6.47 pm on Thursday after a lethal injection at the state prison in Huntsville, Texas. His killings of his baby girl and two toddlers so rocked the city of Brownsville on the US border with Mexico that the apartment building was later demolished – and the land turned into a park and community garden.

After a spiritual adviser prayed over him briefly, Rubio thanked friends and family for their “love and understanding” and said he was “not the same person I once was”. He thanked prison staff for teaching him how to find God and also expressed love for Angela Camacho, his common-law wife who is serving life in prison for her role in the slayings.

“I want to say I’m sorry to my wife’s family,” he said. “I can’t change the past but I can tell you my life is going to be over. But that’s not going to make you happy.”

“I’m going to be with my kids in a little bit,” he added. “I’m ready to go to heaven to be with my lord and savior.”

As the lethal dose of the sedative pentobarbital began taking effect, Rubio took three breaths and began what sounded like snoring. All movement ceased after, and a physician pronounced him dead 21 minutes after the injection began.

Also on Thursday, 73-year-old James Pavatt received a lethal injection at an Oklahoma prison for the fatal shooting of his girlfriend’s estranged husband in 2001. The victim was killed as he arrived to pick up his two young children before Thanksgiving.

Thursday’s executions were the 36th and 37th this year, with the US on track to surpass 40 executions for a second consecutive year – a pace driven by Florida.

The death penalty has drawn renewed attention since the failed execution of Christa Pike in Tennessee in September. And on 5 November, California’s governor, Gavin Newsom, announced he would commute hundreds of death sentences before leaving office in January – what experts say would be the largest mass clemency in American history.

In Texas, Rubio and Camacho were accused of killing three-year-old Julissa Quesada, 14-month-old John E Rubio and two-month-old Mary Jane Rubio in the apartment they shared in Brownsville. Camacho received a life sentence after pleading guilty.

An appeals court overturned Rubio’s first capital murder conviction, ruling Camacho’s statements were improperly admitted as evidence in his trial. At his second trial, Rubio again pleaded not guilty by reason of insanity but again was convicted and sentenced to death.

According to court records, Rubio wasn’t the biological father of the two older children but raised them as his own.

At various points, Rubio claimed the children were possessed and that he was the “chosen one” intended to save the world.

Defense experts diagnosed him with paranoid schizophrenia, a finding prosecution experts rejected. The defense experts also testified his childhood – marked by violence at home, “toxic” parents, drug use and prostitution – damaged him developmentally.

Meanwhile, in Oklahoma, Pavatt was convicted of first-degree murder and conspiracy to commit murder in the killing of Rob Andrew, a 39-year-old advertising executive. Pavatt attended the same church as the Andrew family and had been having an affair with Rob Andrew’s wife, Brenda Andrew. She was convicted at a separate trial and is the only woman currently on Oklahoma’s death row.

Weeks before the shooting, Rob Andrew told police someone was trying to kill him after the brake lines on his car were cut. Brenda Andrew told police after her husband was killed that masked intruders shot her in the arm and fatally shot him.

Pavatt and Brenda Andrew fled to Mexico with her two children after the shooting and were arrested three months later while re-entering the US.

On Thursday, when he was asked if he had any last words before his execution, Pavatt shook his head no. As a chaplain read from the Bible, Pavatt appeared to mouth some words that couldn’t be heard.

Also on Thursday, former Oklahoma death row inmate Richard Glossip pleaded guilty to a lesser charge of being an accessory in a 1997 murder and awaits sentencing in December.

Glossip, who had been on the brink of execution three times, was released on bond in May after nearly 30 years in prison. He had been awaiting a retrial after the US supreme court overturned his conviction.

#127. Somali-American Uber driver brutally assaulted in alleged hate crime in Minnesota

Mohamed Yusuf is fighting for his life after alleged attack by passenger who said he did not like driver’s first name

A Somali-American Uber driver was allegedly brutally assaulted by a passenger in Hopkins, Minnesota, on Saturday, in what his family says “appears to be a hate crime”.

The driver, Mohamed Yusuf, is currently hospitalized and fighting for his life, his family told the Somali Brief, a digital news site run by two Somali American journalists. The driver’s cousin, Abdinasir Ali told the outlet that the alleged attack took place early Saturday after a passenger in Hopkins, a suburban city south-west of Minneapolis, asked Yusuf’s name and then said he did not like the name Mohamed.

The passenger allegedly then choked the driver from behind, took control of the car and used it to assault the driver. “He was run over multiple times with his own vehicle,” Yusuf’s son, Mustafa Osman, told the Somali Brief.

The badly injured driver was later found “a mile down the road in a ditch”, with broken legs and ribs, two collapsed lungs and a spinal injury, his son said.

The Hopkins police department said in a statement they are investigating the assault after officers responded to a report of a suspicious vehicle at 1.20am on Saturday.

“Officers located an adult male suspect nearby and took him into custody,” the police department stated. They noted preliminary information indicates the incident involved an Uber ride share interaction and that the investigation remains ongoing.

“This is a pure hate crime,” one member of Yusuf’s family at the hospital told the Somali Brief. “Our politicians created this hostile environment.”

The alleged assault comes after Donald Trump made racist rhetoric and lies about Somali Americans in Minnesota a central focus of his midterm campaign rallies, and the president’s inflammatory claims have been echoed by Minnesota Republicans.

House majority whip Tom Emmer of Minnesota, recently said on a conservative podcast: “I’ve got basically the Somali civil war that’s come to the state of Minnesota.”

The Minnesota chapter of the Council on American-Islamic Relations (Cair-MN), community leaders, elected officials and ride-share driver groups were planning a press conference on Monday at the Hennepin County Medical Center to call for justice.

“Mohamed Yusuf should be home safe with his family, not fighting for his life after an attack fueled by hatred towards his Somali identity,” said Omar Fateh, a state senator in Minnesota, in a statement on social media. “We need answers and an immediate and transparent investigation.”

SEIU Local 26, a local labor union that organizes rideshare drivers, said in a social media post on the alleged attack: “We are shocked, angered and saddened by the news of the alleged attack that happened this weekend on Mohamed, a Minnesotan and Uber driver.”

A GoFundMe created on behalf of Yusuf and his family has already raised over $140,000. “As a result of this senseless assault, he suffered severe injuries and is now in a coma, fighting for his life,” the family wrote in the appeal for financial support.

“We are shocked and horrified by the details of this senseless attack,” a spokesperson for Uber said in an email.

“We banned the rider from the Uber platform, and we are keeping the driver and his loved ones in our thoughts. We stand ready to assist law enforcement with their investigation.”

#126. Trump’s Oil Grab in Venezuela Is Testing the Country’s National Identity

Long-held beliefs in Venezuela about sovereign control over oil are fraying as the U.S. lays claim to the country’s oil reserves.

Long-held beliefs in Venezuela about sovereign control over oil are fraying as the U.S. lays claim to the country’s oil reserves.

Sunrise over the Cardón Refinery in Punta Cardón, Venezuela. Oil has shaped Venezuela to an extent rare outside the Middle East, becoming a defining feature of the country’s social fabric.Credit...Adriana Loureiro Fernandez for The New York Times

Simon Romero is a former Caracas bureau chief who has covered energy politics across Latin America. Isayen Herrera reports on Venezuela for The New York Times.

Like generations before him, Leonardo Silva grew up steeped in beliefs about the extraordinarily abundant resource that made Venezuela special. All the oil under their feet was theirs, they felt. Virtually free gasoline was a birthright. To be born atop this bounty was like winning the lottery.

Mr. Silva, 38, said his generation viewed oil as a cornerstone of the country’s national identity, when a booming economy allowed Venezuela to occupy a privileged tier in the global order. His children, he said, have a far less rosy view.

“To them oil isn’t important, it’s secondary,” said Mr. Silva, who works at a foundation in Caracas helping people with disabilities. “They don’t have that culture of us being a rich country. They just know that if you don’t work, you don’t eat.”

Oil has shaped Venezuela to an extent that is rare outside the Middle East for more than a century, becoming a defining feature of the social fabric like baseball, beauty pageants or the veneration of the liberation hero Simón Bolívar.

But now the U.S. government is taking control of a huge portion of Venezuela’s oil. And, pressed by economic need, long-held nationalist convictions about oil, starting with the need for sovereign control, are giving way to a more complex web of perspectives.

The ways in which Venezuelans are re-evaluating oil, and by extension their reliance on it, are the result of immense upheaval over the last decade. Oil production plummeted because of mismanagement, political purges, corruption and sanctions.

#125. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#124. Planned asylum seeker detention centre next to Oxfordshire sewage works criticised

Thames Water says detainees at proposed accommodation should expect to experience insect swarms and strong smells

A Home Office plan to build asylum seeker detention facilities next to a sewage pump is facing objections after water company officials warned that detainees should expect to experience insect swarms and strong smells.

Proposed secure accommodation blocks at Campsfield House immigration removal centre in Oxfordshire will be 20 metres from a sewage pumping station, according to a written submission from Thames Water.

A senior development manager at the company wrote that it could give rise to “amenity effects, including odour, noise, vibration, lighting associated with maintenance activities, insects and flies” for any inhabitants. In a previous submission, the company wrote that any development may lead to sewage flooding.

The warnings, which come as the government hopes to expand the immigration detention estate by 40%, have been seized upon by campaigners against the expansion.

Ally Swadling, the lead legal education officer at the charity Right to Remain, said: “Thames Water’s concerns about sewage infrastructure at Campsfield show how little regard is being given to even the most basic standards of sanitation and human dignity.

“They reinforce what we already know – this government is putting its political priorities above people’s lives and wellbeing.”

The centre near Kidlington re-opened in December 2025 after it was shut in 2018 following years of problems, including suicides, riots, escapes and complaints about conditions.

The Home Office is planning to increase the centre’s capacity from 160 bed spaces to 400, and to convert existing buildings. According to the Thames Water submission in October, officials also “raised concerns regarding capacity within the foul water, surface water and water supply networks” in the local area.

A separate 2015 written consultation response, which was submitted in June, states the development may lead to sewage flooding in the area.

“Thames Water request that an impact study be undertaken to ascertain, with a greater degree of certainty, whether the proposed development will lead to overloading of existing infrastructure,” it said.

Written representations, including that of Thames Water, will be heard at a public inquiry beginning on 14 December.

Oxfordshire county council has formally objected to the expansion of Campsfield House, saying it risks intensifying existing public concern, and called for the site to be shut permanently.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

According to the Home Office, nearly 80,000 people have been returned or deported from the UK since the 2024 election to the end of June 2026, an increase of 52% on the previous two years.

Imran Hussain, the director of external affairs at the Refugee Council, said: “Detention is a harmful, dehumanising experience that must only be used as a last resort. Evidence shows it’s ineffective, expensive and damages people forced to endure it.

“Instead of expanding sites such as Campsfield House immigration removal centre, and in this case, in a way that could potentially be detrimental to the health and wellbeing of people staying there, the government must instead use community-based alternatives detention, which have proven to be effective and much cheaper.”

A spokesperson for the Home Office said: “We are confident in our plans for Campsfield immigration removal centre and will continue to engage constructively with the Planning Inspectorate as the process moves forward. We are committed to increasing the removal of those with no right to remain in the UK. Expanding our detention capacity plays an important role in achieving this.”

Thames Water said they had nothing to add to their response.

#121. Burnham pledges early intervention and ‘benefits backstop’ to tackle youth jobs crisis

Prime minister promises ‘root-and-branch rethink’ of support for young people as Milburn report advises sweeping changes to some benefits

Andy Burnham: The crisis facing Britain’s young people is politicians’ fault. To fix it, we need a generational response

Andy Burnham has pledged to intervene as early as childhood to tackle youth unemployment before a watershed review, promising “a root-and-branch rethink of how we support childhood in the modern world”.

Burnham said the government would intervene at all the stages of life in which young people fell behind, and “long before they find themselves out of work and reliant on the state”, including providing more opportunities for vocational training in schools.

Writing in the Guardian, the prime minister said ministers’ response to Alan Milburn’s long-awaited review into the crisis of youth unemployment in the UK would not be “a traditional … reform” but “societal and generational”.

He promised to reduce the number of Neets – 18 to 24-year-olds not in employment, education or training – which topped a million this year for the first time in more than a decade.

Hoping to head off opposition from leftwing MPs in his own party, who rebelled to block Starmer’s welfare changes last summer, Burnham said the government would “invest in our young people and give them a proper chance to get on”, but said the support “will have to come with responsibilities”.

“If we give young people genuine opportunities to learn, train and work, we should expect them to take them where they can. Benefits must become the backstop, not the starting point,” said Burnham.

Milburn’s long-awaited report on youth unemployment will promise almost half a million job and education opportunities within a decade, but will also advise sweeping changes to the benefits system for under-25s.

Some young people will lose their personal independence payments and the right to claim the health element of universal credit, and instead will get a new youth allowance paid at the standard rate of universal credit.

The government has also announced it will expand a taxpayer-funded scheme aimed at helping young people into work to under-25s who have been claiming benefits for a year.

Eligible 18- to 24-year-olds will be “guaranteed” a fully funded, six-month job matched to their skills after 12 months on universal credit. The Liberal Democrats said the six-month offering was worth “very little” without a job at the end of it.

Before the publication of the report, Milburn said the government would have to be “candid with people”, that it would take 10 years for the benefits bill to come down, and investment would have to be made upfront.

Addressing critics calling for the benefits bill to be immediately slashed, the former health secretary told the BBC’s Sunday With Laura Kuenssberg programme: “There’s no such thing as a free lunch. If you are going to will the ends, you’ve got to will the means. I don’t think there’s a single person in this country who believes that it’s tolerable to have one million young people, aged 16 to 24, who are not in education, employment, or training.”

Pat McFadden acknowledged that Milburn’s review would have an “element of compulsion” for Neets and there would be people who would receive less in benefits than currently.

The work and pensions secretary said the UK had to become more like countries that had dealt with youth unemployment more successfully, such as the Netherlands, where the youth desk would “literally knock on your door” and offer options of training or work.

But he denied that Milburn was proposing a ban on young people claiming health-related benefits. “That’s not what he’s saying. He’s saying if you have a long-term disability, that means you can never work,” he said.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

“You will actually get more than you will now. He’s saying that if you have extra costs, there will be a benefit you can apply to for extra costs. He’s also saying there should be a local fund for short-term needs, and for other people, he’s saying there should be more support to get into work.

Change was necessary, he added, because the number of young people claiming personal independence payments (Pip) had doubled since the start of the decade, while the number of young people on the health element of universal credit had increased by 80%.

Burnham hit out at the Conservatives and Reform, who have called for deep and immediate cuts to welfare, with Kemi Badenoch vowing to slash the bill by £36.1bn by 2030 if the party wins the next general election.

Under the plans 70% of child benefit disability-related claims linked to ADHD and “milder” behavioural disorders and learning disabilities would be cut, with Labour calling the proposals “a charter for human misery”.

The shadow work and pensions secretary, Helen Whately, acknowledged the plans could mean around half a million children losing disability-related payments.

“This is to help those children,” she told the BBC’s Sunday With Laura Kuenssberg, adding that under the plans, child mental health services would receive £1bn of funding. “The point of that funding is because there are children who are struggling. Let’s use this funding in the best possible way to support those children.”

Burnham accused past Conservative governments of taking “a deliberate decision to make young people bear the brunt” of austerity cuts. “What is the Tory solution now? To punish those young people once again,” he said.

“The crude cuts they are proposing … would simply compound the mistakes they made in government. They would drive children into poverty and increase levels of family homelessness even further, piling further pressure on councils, the NHS and other public services. This is not “reform”. It is a path to higher public spending and broken services.”

#118. Labour rebellion over Rosebank oilfield expected to top 100 MPs

Total of 75 have already signed letter to Burnham opposing plans as discontent also grows over Europe and welfare changes

As many as 100 Labour MPs could sign a letter opposing the giant Rosebank oilfield, as discontent among backbenchers looks likely to surge this week, with rebellions also brewing over Europe and welfare changes.

A total of 75 MPs have already signed the private letter, which is expected to be delivered in the next fortnight to Andy Burnham as part of a campaign in which Labour MPs from the party’s 2024 intake are playing a significant role.

While the energy secretary, Miatta Fahnbulleh, is said to be minded to reject the controversial oilfield, she could still be overruled by the prime minister, amid a cabinet split in which the chancellor, John Healy, is in favour of the plans.

The letter, first reported by the Sunday Times, says Britain’s credibility as a global climate leader would be destroyed if drilling went ahead off the Scottish coast, and argues that it would not reduce energy bills.

“We’re at a point where Reform and the Greens are on the back foot and Labour is enjoying a bounce, but a lot of people are very worried about Rosebank,” said one Labour MP who signed the letter.

“Andy’s big problem is also that he has let three fights start at the same time, which could prove to be very awkward. Rows about this and what our position should be on European relations have come at the same time as the debates that will come this week over [Alan Milburn’s] proposals on out-of-work benefits for young people. If control of the narrative on one issue is lost it could lead to panic about another.”

Another Labour MP said: “The interest in this is not just from the 2024 MPs. There are a good number of colleagues who just don’t sign letters like this normally, but who are concerned. It’s about staying true to what we told voters, and the fact that a lot of people after the summer we’ve had are really now making a link between the climate crisis and what their experience was when it comes to temperatures or flooding.

“The prospect of government resignations also looms if the drilling licence is granted. While the foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, is unlikely to go, such a decision would also put [him] in an awkward position given his past description of Rosebank as a ‘colossal waste of taxpayer money and climate vandalism’.”

Instrumental in organising the letter is Sera, an affiliate and member-led environmental organisation within Labour, whose members include Miliband as well as ministers such as Luke Pollard, Georgia Gould, Matthew Pennycook and others.

Some who have signed the letter include those who had reluctantly backed the smaller Jackdaw field on the basis that it was primarily gas.

The company operating both fields said on Sunday that gas had begun to flow from the Jackdaw platform, “bringing vital UK energy resources ashore in time for winter”.

Claire Mueller, Jackdaw project director at the North Sea oil and gas producer Adura, said Rosebank and Jackdaw were expected to generate £1.4bn in tax revenues before the end of this parliament, rising to £3.8bn by the end of the next.

“With Jackdaw now producing, and other major projects including Rosebank in our pipeline, we have an opportunity to build a bright future for the North Sea, supporting jobs, investment and Britain’s energy security for years to come,” she added.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Heavy backing for Rosebank is also coming from Labour-supporting unions the GMB and Unite, which have considerable influence over Burnham’s operation, and who are also expressing concern at his talk of rejoining the EU.

The letter opposing Rosebank is modelled on the one that derailed Keir Starmer’s welfare overhaul plans, which spanned the new intake and veterans. Yet one of its signatories pointed out stark differences.

“I do think it will probably get to around 100 MPs and may go beyond it, but the welfare rebels were willing to be much more ruthless. The other thing is that some of the people behind that rebellion are now in government and they are wise to these things.”

The government has described their process around Rosebank as “quasi-judicial”. Fahnbulleh previously told the Guardian that the decision would be consistent with the UK’s climate obligations, carbon budgets and the energy transition.

#116. Trident nuclear submarine concludes record underwater deployment

HMS Vengeance’s seven-month patrol prompts fears over slow pace of maintenance of ageing fleet and strain on crew

A Trident nuclear submarine has concluded a record seven-month deployment, returning to the Faslane naval base in Scotland where its crew was greeted by the defence secretary, Wes Streeting.

HMS Vengeance began its patrol on 12 April and spent 217 days submerged before returning on Sunday, having left the country when Keir Starmer was in Downing Street and Manchester United were third in the Premier League.

Its tour surpasses the previous 205-day record set by HMS Vanguard, and has raised questions about the strains placed on sailors and the reliability of ageing submarines sent on the extended missions.

“Patrols are getting longer and longer,” said David Cullen, a nuclear expert with the Basic thinktank. “The fleet was designed around three-month tours, and that was considered to be a long time to be stuck underwater in a metal box with no contact with the outside world.”

The increase in the time spent at sea reflects the slow pace of submarine maintenance, prompting Labour to blame spending cuts implemented by the Conservatives, though naval chiefs insist there is a plan for improvement.

Streeting said the length of time the crew had spent submerged did not “sit lightly” with him.

Streeting, who was accompanied in Faslane by the first secretary of state, Lou Haigh, added: “It makes me feel even more indebted to the crew for the service they’ve given to the country, for the commitment they show, the dedication, the professionalism, the expertise.

“I think the whole country can be extraordinarily proud of our submariners and of our nuclear deterrent.”

He described the nuclear patrols as the “ultimate national endeavour” and thanked the returning submariners for their “dedicated, heroic service” which he said was the model of patriotism.

HMS Vengeance is one of four submarines that carryBritain’s Trident nuclear missiles. A boat is at sea at all times, so the UK could launch strikes even if land-based systems had been destroyed in a nuclear war.

When the Vanguard class of submarines was brought into service in the 1990s, they were designed to carry three to four months of food for the crew of about 140, and deployments of more than 100 days were considered highly exceptional. Missions now start with tinned food crammed into corridors and other available spaces.

Each submarine has to keep its location secret so it cannot be targeted, meaning it also cannot be resupplied. The boats maintain radio silence and so the crew cannot message out on the long deployments, though they can receive news and other information.

Such is the length of the patrols that the return of a Trident submarine, known as a “day zero”, has become a political event. Starmer made an unannounced visit when the last patrol ended in April, praising the crew on their return.

There have been worries about the psychological impact of the patrols on the crew members, who have to spend seven months underwater in a boat 150 metres long, at a time when the Royal Navy is struggling to recruit new personnel.

Last week, the head of the Royal Navy, Gen Sir Gwyn Jenkins, said he had “nothing but tremendous respect” for the Trident sailors, but added that the UK needed to “improve our submarine maintenance” to allow faster turnaround.

The submarine service, in line with the rest of the navy, has a “no touching rule” – prohibiting physical intimacy on board – though the service has had to contend with allegations of sexual abuse and harassment during deployments.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

In October 2024, the then first sea lord was forced to issue an apology to women – who make up about 10% of the navy – who had suffered “misogyny, bullying and other unacceptable behaviours” in the submarine service.

The navy conducted an inquiry after a whistleblower, Sophie Brook, complained of a “constant campaign of sexual bullying” from male sailors. Reports of rape, physical and verbal abuse were examined in a heavily redacted report.

Trident submarines can carry 40 nuclear warheads, with enough destructive power to destroy Moscow and several other major Russian cities, and a letter from the prime minister to tell the captain how to proceed in the event it is determined the UK no longer exists after an all-out nuclear attack.

Such is the length of the boat’s patrol it will still be carrying Starmer’s letter, though the replacement submarine on rotation will have Andy Burnham’s instructions, written in longhand in the days after he took over at Downing Street.

In July, Labour announced a £26bn, 10-year plan to invest in Britain’s three main naval bases, at Faslane, the operational home of the Trident submarines, Devonport and Portsmouth, as part of a wider increase in defence spending.

However, the first of the replacements for the four Vanguard class submarines, HMS Dreadnought, is not expected to be ready until about 2032, meaning that the old boats have at least five more years to remain in service.

“The inherent risks of operating submarines are being pushed by them being run as they are. At the end of the submarines’ design life they should be run gently, not as hard as they are,” Cullen said.

#114. ‘Pull back the curtain’ on treatment of sexual assault victims in court, says UK government adviser

Professor urges ministers to use awareness triggered by Russell Brand conviction to examine use of rape myths in trials

The debate triggered by high-profile cases must “pull back the curtain” on the treatment of people making allegations of sexual offences in the criminal justice system in England and Wales, the government’s independent adviser on rape has told the Guardian.

Prof Katrin Hohl, who has advised the government in an overhaul of how rape is treated by police forces and the Crown Prosecution Service (CPS), said the government should use the public awareness triggered by Russell Brand’s conviction for rape to examine the use of rape myths and jury direction in trials.

“So many things are going wrong in our justice system when it comes to the treatment of victims of rape and sexual assault, but most of the time no one ever finds out what has happened during the investigation process or during a trial,” she said.

Hohl said that despite “significant progress” made by police forces and the CPS – who have been directed to make investigations more focused on suspects rather than victim behaviour under a system known as Operation Soteria – the court system was being left behind.

A report published by Hohl last month suggested sexual assault cases prepared under the new system were experiencing “a cliff edge” when they came to trial, and were potentially coming up against practices such as the use of rape myths by defence barristers.

“We essentially know very little about how rape and sexual assault cases play out in England and Wales and it is time to pull back the curtain,” said Hohl.

“We need proper research into the actions of barristers, judges and juries during trials so that victims can actually get the justice the law says they should achieve.”

Hohl’s remarks come after the victims commissioner, Claire Waxman, called for a new legal duty on judges to direct juries, if rape myths have been ventured during a trial, stating: “Justice must be grounded in evidence, not misconceptions.”

During the Brand trial, which saw the comedian sentenced to more than 10 years in prison after he was found guilty of raping one woman and sexually assaulting three others, his barrister, Ian Winter, said Brand’s accusers had not “resisted or screamed or even tried to leave”.

Government ministers are also understood to be considering calls for a “staggered” consent law, which would make it illegal for adults over a certain age to have sex with 16- and 17-year-olds, after a woman who alleges she was raped by Brand when she was 16 launched a campaign for the law to be changed.

Mia Latter, who says she was in an abusive three-month relationship with Brand when he was 30 and she was 16, told the Sunday Times: “I think we all sort of agree that … adults shouldn’t be allowed to have sexual relationships with children, with somebody under the age of 18. That really doesn’t seem like a gigantic leap to take.”

On Sunday, Pat McFadden said ministers would discuss the issue. Speaking to the BBC’s Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg, the work and pensions secretary said questions over a staggered age of consent have “been raised and will be discussed”, adding: “[I] can’t tell you what the conclusion of that would be, but the last week has certainly put that up in lights.”

Since the trial, several support services for victims of sexual assault and rape said they had been experiencing a significant increase in the number of calls and messages to helplines.

Rape Crisis centres are experiencing “very significant demand” and phonelines have been inundated with calls since Brand was convicted of rape, said Ciara Bergman, chief executive of Rape Crisis England & Wales.

Many women are also now questioning their own experiences with older men, she said, after separate allegations were made public that David Walliams preyed on young women for sex.

Earlier this week, it was announced that a series of “serious” sexual offence allegations against Walliams were being looked at by the Metropolitan police after an investigation by the Guardian and ITV News. One woman described her alleged encounter with Walliams when she was 15 as “sexual assault”. Walliams denies any wrongdoing.

Bergman said calls to the 24/7 rape and sexual abuse support line had quadrupled since the start of the month, while rape crisis centres were reporting a massive rise in demand for help.

“That demand comes from a range of places,” she said. “There are people who see the news and are reminded of their own experiences and people who are retraumatised by seeing this issue in the news.

“But there are people who may have experienced predation and exploitation at vulnerable times in their lives, and they’re now beginning to question their own experiences.”

She echoed calls for greater transparency in trials, and argued the coverage of high profile cases had highlighted the gap in the public knowledge around the experience of going to trial and issues around consent.

“Our centres are adept at responding to victims but they are under very significant pressure,” she said. “When you look at these stories and the response to them there is no question about the need for more comprehensive support and more rape crisis centres so victims and survivors can get the justice they deserve.”

A Ministry of Justice spokesperson said: “We are always looking at ways we can improve how the justice system treats victims of sexual violence.

“That’s why we are changing the law to stop irrelevant evidence that relies on myths being weaponised against victims, rolling out specialist courtrooms for rape and serious sexual offences and improving support for victims, backed by our record £550m investment in support services.”

Support for anyone affected by sexual abuse issues is available in the UK from Rape Crisis on 0808 500 2222 in England and Wales, 0808 801 0302 in Scotland, or 0800 0246 991 in Northern Ireland. The National Association for People Abused in Childhood (Napac) offers support for adult survivors on 0808 801 0331. Samaritans can be contacted on freephone 116 123. Other international helplines are available for people affected by sexual abuse and childhood abuse, and people in crisis.

#112. Ukraine’s Starshield Access Is Being Restricted Again, Zelensky Says

President Volodymyr Zelensky said the system, which Ukraine uses to guide drones, was being throttled over Russian-occupied territory. SpaceX, its developer, has denied the claim.

Ukrainian military operations were disrupted again this week along the front lines because of restrictions on Starshield, the satellite internet system developed by Elon Musk’s SpaceX and controlled by the U.S. government, President Volodymyr Zelensky said on Friday.

Mr. Zelensky told reporters that speed limits of about 100 miles per hour were being enforced for Ukrainian devices connected to the system. It would be the second time in recent weeks that Ukraine’s Starshield connectivity had been throttled. The previous restrictions caused Ukrainian drones to lose their connection to the system if they flew above certain speeds.

Mr. Zelensky said that the latest speed limits took effect on Monday and were being enforced over Russian-occupied territories, not over all of Ukraine. That hurts Ukraine’s ability to strike Russian troops with drones in those territories.

Imposing restrictions on occupied territories is “very dangerous, because it already has political implications,” Mr. Zelensky said. “If you impose restrictions there, you are already partially recognizing the temporarily occupied territories as not ours,” he added.

Mr. Zelensky said separately in his nightly speech that the Starshield limits hurt Ukraine’s ability to use newly developed interceptors to protect its cities from Russia’s jet-powered drones. It is likely, however, that those limits would not affect interceptors in Kyiv, the capital, which is being battered by jet-powered drones but is far from the front lines.

Mr. Musk and SpaceX did not respond to requests for comment on Friday evening. SpaceX had earlier told Axios that the Ukrainian claim about throttled connectivity was false.

#109. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.
