#86. How a US extradition bid over Hamas allegations put a wealthy activist before a Spanish court

James ‘Fergie’ Chambers, a Cox Enterprises heir, faced a two-hour hearing about alleged support for Hamas

A two-hour hearing in Spain’s high court Friday on the US extradition request of wealthy leftwing activist and philanthropist James “Fergie” Chambers turned out to be a study in contrasts.

Chambers’s defense took most of the session to query the US citizen and detail irregularities in the Trump administration’s extradition request – several of which the Guardian has reported on – as well as the figures of Spanish law that would lead the three-judge panel to deny the request; while the prosecutor sped through his case, head down, asking Chambers no questions at all.

Notably, neither the judges or the prosecutor asked Chambers to address the insinuation in the extradition request that the wealthy activist had supported Hamas. Chambers’s case is the first known instance of the US seeking to extradite a citizen over alleged support for Hamas, an accusation apparently tied to an international money laundering charge and donations to organizations helping Palestinians in Gaza.

But the defense’s queries did allow him to speak for the first time since his 10 July arrest in Ibiza about events at the center of the case: 2023 protests against Israeli weapons manufacturer Elbit in Massachusets and New Hampshire and 2024 transfers of $7.2m to Tunisia, where he lived at the time. The latter was buttressed with testimony from a financial expert.

On the whole, Chambers painted a picture different from the depiction contained in the extradition request, that of a violent backer of Hamas on the run from the US. At one point, he noted that his family’s next door neighbor in Tunis was the US ambassador.

The court will decide next week on extraditing Chambers. If the decision is affirmative, Spain’s council of ministers can reverse the court’s decision. This means Chambers’s extradition may be among the last decisions made under Pedro Sánchez, the socialist prime minister, before national elections on 29 November.

Friday’s hearing began with Chambers’s defense asking about his wealth. A self-described communist and anti-imperialist, Chambers sold stock back to his family’s business, Atlanta-based Cox Enterprises, worth about $250m in mid-2023.

Then he started a foundation, he told the court. He and the foundation’s three-member board decide on funding and ensure that no projects seeking support are under US sanctions, Chambers said. The foundation has funded an estimated 100 progressive and humanitarian projects in 20 countries, according to his partner, activist and actor Stella Schnabel – including, Chambers said Friday, organizations in Gaza working on issues such as “medical treatment for children, transportation out of dangerous areas and clean water and food”.

Two of the request’s three charges – riot and conspiracy to riot – are related to the late-2023 protests against Elbit.

Chambers detailed his participation in the protests: locking himself to a door, marching in the street and – in New Hampshire – standing in the road, speaking to the media and paying bail for four protesters who were eventually found guilty of misdemeanor charges and served 30 days in jail.

As for the $7.2m, the defense and a financial expert serving as a witness laid out how ten bank transfers from the US to Tunisia mostly went to sponsor the football club Club Africain – described by Chambers as “one of the largest football clubs in Africa” – and a sporting business Chambers launched while living in Tunisia.

“Is there any indication that the money went to support terrorist activities?” asked defense attorney Aitor Martínez. “None,” said financial expert Albert Deulofeu Schilt.

Then, in about 15 minutes of rapid-fire monotone, prosecutor Juan Hernández Villalba laid out a wholly different picture of Chambers as backing “an organized structure … aimed at repeatedly committing illegal acts … together with funds destined for terrorist organizations”.

He accused the defense of laying out the facts of the case “in a fragmented way … when what’s most relevant is an evaluation of the whole picture”.

“The extradition petition isn’t based on ideology or political statements, but on actions [Chambers] organized, financed and carried out, causing material damage,” Hernández Villalba asserted.

The actions against Elbit “were presented as a simple protest”, he said. “Nevertheless, the actions described by the US were planned for days … with objectively violent content.”

Given all this, the prosecutor said, “there are more than enough indications to consider … that [Chambers’s] funding was aimed at terrorism”.

Among the 100-plus people in varying time zones following Friday’s hearing on the court’s YouTube channel was Irish film-maker and journalist Sinéad O’Shea, whose documentary on Chambers called All About the Money makes its New York premiere this weekend. It covers the time period cited by the FBI affidavit underpinning the US request. O’Shea said that extradition case “builds a false narrative which begins with the protest in New Hampshire – a piece of vandalism at worst and for which people ended up serving 30 days. I saw myself the football club in which he invested in Tunisia and his donations to Palestinian and other charities are a matter of public record. It is impossible not to see this as a politically motivated case.”

Chambers’s views have been extensively profiled in US media. A week after the 7 October 2023 Hamas attacks on Israel, he posted on X about Israel’s response: “Stop calling it ‘collective punishment’/There’s nothing to be ‘punished’ for/No faction of the Palestinian resistance, Hamas or other, has done *anything* wrong/It’s simply genocide/Like it has been.”

Dressed in dark jeans, a grey-blue shirt, a black jacket, with a close-cropped haircut and mustache, Chambers got the last word during the hearing.

“To this day I cannot understand why I have been in prison since July for what amounts to speaking emotionally and committing minor offenses of public disturbance,” he said.

“I’ve never had any contact with any terrorist organization. I’ve certainly never financed any terrorist organization … and for this my family has been torn apart. I missed my grandmother’s funeral last week. My five-year-old son asks where I am every day. And my wife will give birth, God willing, in March – and I don’t know if I will be by her side.”

“[All] because I have spoken my mind, and I had a lot more money than others who have spoken their mind, in my country. And that’s all it amounts to.”

#85. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#83. Hundreds of thousands of Czechs protest in Prague over media freedom

Estimated 350,000 people rally against plan to scrap licence fees and fund broadcasters from state budget

Hundreds of thousands of Czechs have rallied in Prague to protest against a perceived government attempt to stifle media freedom.

The gathering on Sunday to save the mandatory licence fees that fund Czechia’s public broadcasters drew more than 350,000 people, making it one of the biggest in the country’s history, organisers said.

Many people wore triangular paper hats similar to an animated TV character called Večerníček, a children’s bedtime story mascot who has become a symbol of the protest. Others carried banners saying: “We won’t give media away.”

The event was held at the capital’s Letná Plain, the cradle of the 1989 Velvet Revolution which ended more than 40 years of communist rule in the country.

It marked the latest challenge to the prime minister, Andrej Babiš, and his coalition of populist, hard-right nationalist and Eurosceptic parties, and follows weeks of demonstrations in towns and cities and a strike by staff at Czech TV and Czech Radio.

Protesters say the plan to scrap licence fees and fund the broadcasters from the state budget would give ministers more leverage over newsrooms and follows the example of Hungary, Poland, Serbia and Slovakia, where current and previous rightwing populist governments eroded media independence.

A series of protests has been held since late October, when the first reading of the bill to scrap the licence fees was pushed through in the middle of the night.

The Million Moments for Democracy group, which organised the demonstration, said the authorities would now grasp the level of opposition. “We do witness here something extraordinary today,” said Mikuláš Minář, the lead organiser. “It’s just a beginning.”

In a statement, the group demanded that the government withdraw its plan and expressed concern that Babiš and his coalition partners posed a threat to democracy. “Babiš and his government obviously think they will get away with anything … that they can ignore experts, silence critics and treat democratic institutions like their property,” the group said.

“We refuse the Slovak scenario,” said one protest banner. “Hands off the media,” said another.

Šárka Dumišincová, a singer, said for her the media represented freedom. “The fact that someone here is trying to control that freedom is unacceptable to me. That’s why I’m here today,” she told the Associated Press.

“We need to keep media independent. The government is taking steps to control them, cut their funding and affect what they broadcast,” computer programmer Jan Snasel told AFP at the rally.

The proposed changes, which would cut state television and radio budgets by about 15%, have drawn fire from international media organisations.

The European Broadcasting Union, the European Federation of Journalists, Reporters Without Borders and the International Press Institute have also criticised the government’s media plan, likening it to controversial moves by Slovakia’s prime minister, Robert Fico, and Hungary’s former premier Viktor Orbán.

Babiš, a billionaire who returned to power last year, said protesters were riling up the public with unfounded claims about threats to media freedom.

Last week, he published an op-ed on what he called the “seven lies” being peddled by the opposition over the licence fees, which together add up to 205 koruna (£7) a month.

“Anyone who talks about ‘nationalisation’ [of public media] is deliberately lying,” he wrote, describing the criticism as “hysterical outbursts”. “Under no circumstances will we interfere in editorial work.”

Babiš heads a coalition that includes his ANO, or YES, movement, the rightwing Motorists for Themselves and the anti-migrant and pro-Russian Freedom and Direct Democracy party. Despite the protests, ANO still enjoys the backing of about 30% in opinion polls.

#81. Estonia to close all border crossings with Russia after attack on drone maker

Authorities say Russian intelligence service ordered attack which Estonian PM links to Tallinn’s support for Ukraine

Estonia will close all its border crossings with Russia from 1 December after a suspected sabotage attack on a drone maker in the Baltic state over the weekend, which authorities said was ordered by Russia’s GRU military intelligence service.

On Saturday morning, eight people were detained during an attempted attack on Threod Systems, a defence company that manufactures long-range attack and reconnaissance drones. No injuries or damage were reported after the company’s security team responded to the incident.

Estonia’s internal security service, Kapo, said on Sunday the attack was carried out “with the aim of causing an explosion and setting the building on fire”. Authorities have requested a court order for five of those detained to remain in custody, Estonia’s public broadcaster ERR reported.

The Estonian prime minister, Kristen Michal, said the border closure was needed to reduce the risk of further attacks and “send a clear message that attacks on Estonia’s defence industry and defence capabilities” were unacceptable.

He linked the attacks with Estonia’s continuing support for Ukraine. “Russia’s aim is to make us afraid, make us hesitate and weaken our support for Ukraine,” Michal said.

“Estonia’s response is the opposite. We will strengthen our security, work even more closely with businesses and our allies, increase pressure on Russia and continue supporting Ukraine.”

The Estonian foreign minister, Margus Tsahkna, echoed the warning, saying: “Russia must understand that hostile actions against Estonia come with consequences. Attempts to intimidate, destabilise or undermine our security will not go unanswered.”

Estonia operates three border crossings with Russia – Narva in the north, and Koidula and Luhamaa in the south. The authorities did not specify how long the closure would be.

It is the second attempted attack on an Estonian defence company that authorities have attributed to Russia, after an arson attack at Milrem Robotics in August.

In September, Kapo said it was confident that the incident was “a deliberate and pre-planned attack carried out on behalf of Russian special services”.

The agency also said it fitted a pattern of Russian attacks targeting defence and drone companies, particularly those with links to Ukraine.

At the time Estonia summoned Russia’s chargé d’affaires over the attack, but the Kremlin denied any link to Russia, saying it was a “baseless” and “ill-considered” accusation “with absolutely no relation to reality”.

Several European intelligence services have repeatedly warned of potential attacks on defence companies and growing Russian attempts to recruit citizens in EU and Nato countries – on online messaging platforms – to carry out acts of sabotage.

Michal noted that the weekend’s arrests came after security agencies told the defence industry of potential risks. “We do not warn about these threats without reason. Preparedness matters, and this time it clearly paid off,” he said.

The Russian foreign ministry spokesperson, Maria Zakharova, told state news agency Tass: “If ‌these [border closures] are ‌their real plans, then ‌they fit into yet another bout of aggressive and unrestrained Russophobia.”

#79. In Europe, an Emboldened Iran Contributes to a Sense of Anxiety

Prosecutors and government officials believe Iran or its proxies are behind a series of security breaches and threats in Britain and Europe, unsettling the region.

On Veterans Day this past week, President Trump declared that his decision to go to war with Iran had been a complete success.

“When this conflict is over, and it will be soon,” he said, speaking on a blustery morning from Arlington National Cemetery outside Washington, “the entire world will be a much safer and better place, more peaceful place.”

A growing number of attempted terrorist attacks across the continent and in Britain, including a foiled plot against a major American military base, arson attacks on Jewish community sites and cyberattacks that raised temperatures in refrigerators and blood banks are rattling national security officials and unnerving the public. Many have been attributed to Iran or to proxies acting on its behalf.

The sense of global unease was heightened when the U.S. government grounded all air travel for hours on Friday in what officials later said was a technical issue.

European residents had already grown frustrated before the most recent round of turmoil. The economic consequences of the war that Mr. Trump and the Israeli government started against Iran in February have piled up: Gas prices are higher because of disruptions of oil deliveries through the Strait of Hormuz. Groceries cost more. Heating homes in winter has become far less affordable.

And in recent weeks, the effect has grown darker as security officials have accused Iran or its proxies of conducting a series of terrorism-related plots. In September, U.S. prosecutors say, an Iraqi man, an Iranian operative and a senior operative for an Iran proxy group in a New York jail plotted to attack R.A.F. Fairford, an air base in England used by American bombers. They did not succeed, but the effort prompted a major security incident.

#75. Prince Harry tells Ukrainians they are ‘going to win war’ on surprise trip to Lviv

Duke of Sussex meets wounded veterans in city and says country could one day host Invictus Games

Prince Harry has told Ukrainian soldiers they are going to win the war with Russia, during a surprise visit to the country.

The Duke of Sussex met wounded veterans and medics in the city of Lviv, in the west of Ukraine, as he addressed a conference on treating those injured in war. He said he had taken a risk in travelling to the country, but that he had felt “warmth” when arriving on Saturday.

It is the British army veteran’s fourth visit to Ukraine since Russia’s invasion in February 2022.

He spoke at a conference organised by the Superhumans Center, a clinic in Lviv that specialises in care and rehabilitation of those who have fought in the conflict. The prince has previously supported the group, which provides prosthetic limbs to those who have lost them in battle.

He said: “Every single time I come back to Ukraine, I feel welcome, I feel warmth. You’re going to win the war and then you’re going to be set up for success.”

Harry watched wheelchair rugby players who hope to be in Ukraine’s team at next year’s Invictus Games, which will take place in Birmingham. Ukraine is aiming to send about 60 competitors to the event – its largest ever team.

The prince said the games for wounded military veterans, which he set up in 2014, could one day be held in Ukraine. “When the war is over and when Ukraine wins, we will be ready to support you in holding an Invictus Games,” he promised.

At the rugby match, Harry hugged a player he recognised – Valery Odainyk, who took part in the Invictus Games in Canada in 2025. The 41-year-old, who uses prosthetic legs, said he was “still an active serviceman”, and the prince expressed surprise he found time to train.

Odainyk said afterwards he had told Harry his involvement in his sport “all started and continues with the Invictus Games”.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

The prince visited the Ukrainian capital, Kyiv, in September 2025 and in April this year. During the April visit, he called Ukraine “a country bravely and successfully defending Europe’s eastern flank” and said “it matters that we don’t lose sight of the significance of that”.

The 42-year-old spent a decade in the British army and served in Afghanistan, including as a helicopter pilot. He has described those years as the happiest of his life.

#74. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#72. After a summer win, India's Gen Z protesters face a winter of uncertainty

India's Gen Z protesters are facing the limits of dissent

Cheers erupted at Delhi's Jantar Mantar protest site in July as news broke that Gen-Z protests had forced federal education minister Dharmendra Pradhan to resign.

One protester turned to me, beaming: "This is the youth's victory."

Just over three months later, the mood has shifted dramatically. Student protesters are now struggling even to assemble. Demonstrations by the Gen Z-led Cockroach Janta Party (CJP) last month over voting rights were met with sweeping restrictions and thousands of peaceful protesters were detained.

The summer had seen a new wave of dissent in India, driven by deep frustration among the youth and fuelled by irreverent online satire.

The movement spread across the country, and Prime Minister Narendra Modi, a leader with a strongman image, unaccustomed to open criticism or mockery, struggled to contain it. But the government's increasingly forceful security response suggests it has learned from its rare concession to protesters, invoking public order to curb dissent.

The ruling Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) national spokesperson Syed Zafar Islam rejected accusations of heavy-handedness and told the BBC: "The right to protest and freedom of expression is mandated by the constitution, but it has to be done within the law. It cannot override the rights of others, at the cost of public security".

This is by no means the first time the Modi government has faced large-scale protests.

But the breadth of them pose a challenge for the BJP, according to Yamini Aiyar, former president of the Centre for Policy Research and visiting professor at Brown University.

The CJP led massive protests in July which ended with the resignation of a federal minister

"They are not defined by caste, gender or economic class," Aiyar said. "Even some of the core BJP base, the upwardly mobile, lower-income classes, were attracted by the movement."

The scale of the youth protests is also magnified by its online presence. Protesters have broken a longstanding political taboo, openly mocking the prime minister online.

"It's more than just laughs," said Sakhi, a student protester who identifies as they/them and affiliated with one of Delhi University's left-wing student organisations.

"The fear of repercussions in other generations isn't felt as much in ours," they said. "Comedy is a new language to challenge authority."

Previous protest movements posed serious challenges to Modi but were easier to dismiss as representing narrow sections of society or to label "anti-national", according to Aiyar.

In 2020, farmers protesting against controversial agricultural reforms were blocked from entering Delhi, leading to months of demonstrations and, at times, clashes on the capital's borders. After a year of protests, the government abandoned the reforms.

Protests against a contentious citizenship law in 2019 and 2020 are a stark reminder of the powers of the judiciary should anyone be deemed a national security threat.

Student activist Umar Khalid was arrested under anti-terror laws after violent clashes in Delhi that killed 53 people, mostly Muslims. He was accused of being a "key conspirator". He has now spent more than six years in jail without his trial having begun.

His partner Banojyotsna Lahiri told the BBC they have no regrets, but that the situation instilled "a kind of fear that has prevailed".

The CJP in its early days became a unifier for Indian youth frustrated with the state of education, jobs and economy

"We are just looking at a very dark mesh of empty space, because we don't know when the endpoint is," Lahiri said. Khalid's latest appeal was lodged this month.

Aiyar believes opponents are using a familiar "playbook" today to "de-legitimise" protests, but that it isn't resonating as convincingly.

Nevertheless, the youth protests have struggled to recapture this summer's momentum.

CJP leaders, including spokesperson Saurav Das, have faced repeated detentions and legal action. Das and fellow leader Ashutosh Ranka spent a week in Delhi's Tihar jail after being accused of trespassing at Jantar Mantar.

The site of their summer victory has since been struck off the list of authorised protest venues, a decision being challenged in court.

Protesters from other groups, including the left-wing All India Students' Association (Aisa), have also faced police action over allegations of defying orders.

"We were expecting some kind of crackdown," said Das. He added that "the government has learnt a lot" since the summer.

Legal experts warn the proceedings themselves can have a chilling effect.

"A lot of cases can lead to acquittals, but it can take years. The process is the punishment, and that can deter protesters," said Ali Khan, an assistant professor at Lloyd Law College.

The restrictions on demonstrations have become excessive, according to constitutional lawyer Vrinda Grover, who is advising the CJP.

Tens of thousands of people gathered at Delhi's iconic Ramlila Maidan for the opposition protest on 1 November

"Those who are organising the protests have repeatedly emphasised that this is non-violent," she argued. "Rights have been whittled down in the name of law and order."

The BJP has rejected suggestions it is suppressing peaceful dissent.

Islam told the BBC that demonstrations "cannot override the rights of other citizens just because a handful of people want to protest and be responsible for violence".

Even dissenting online carries risk. Sakhi's viral social media posts have attracted targeted abuse and transphobic attacks. The BBC has seen posts threatening them with violence and rape.

Sometimes, it can deter Sakhi from protesting or even make them change the way they dress.

"Making people laugh can turn into being called anti-national," Sakhi said. "They want me to feel damaged," they added.

They claim some of their content was removed after getting millions of views.

Under India's information technology laws, the government can order platforms to block content on national security grounds. Notices on X and Instagram have shown accounts being withheld in India.

CJP leaders have likened the crackdown to the 1975 Emergency, when then prime minister Indira Gandhi suspended civil liberties and jailed political opponents.

Opposition parties have, in part, filled the void. Tens of thousands gathered in Delhi on 1 November for a rally organised by INDIA bloc, an alliance of opposition parties led by Congress, and permitted by the authorities.

CJP leaders, meanwhile, insist the movement is bigger than their organisation.

When Das walked out Delhi's Tihar prison on 21 October, photographs showed him smiling.

"I would just say wait and watch," Das told us. "Time is on our side. We are a very young generation."

#70. Jojo the gorilla has spent his life alone. Can he finally find a companion?

Jojo the gorilla has spent his life alone. Can he finally find a companion?

In 2015, a young male gorilla named Jojo took his first tentative steps onto grass.

Until that moment, the 11-year-old ape had spent almost his entire life in a tiny cage at a private zoo in Gabon, after being taken from the wild as an infant. His family had been killed by the poachers who took him.

More than a decade later, Jojo is settled into a sanctuary â but he still lives alone. He hasn't had a family since he was taken, or indeed any gorilla friends.

Jojo's carers at the Lekedi Foundation in Gabon want to find a companion - possibly another rescued or zoo-bred gorilla â that Jojo could live and bond with. But finding him a friend - and bringing them into his life - is not straightforward.

"He's probably the most extreme case of isolation I've ever seen in a great ape," says Prof Gillian Forrester from the University of Sussex, who is studying Jojo as part of research into great ape psychology and how to help rescued animals recover from trauma.

Jojo's keeper Maxe Anaclet Makaba: 'He definitely feels lonely - you can see it in his eyes'

Jojo was less than a year old â an infant clinging to his mother â when he was captured and sold.

Most gorillas spend the first 14 to 19 years of their life in an embedded family unit but he was kept completely alone and on public display at the private zoo that bought him.

Maxe Anaclet Makaba, who is head keeper at Lekedi and sees Jojo nearly every day, says it is clear he "craves human interaction".

"He will even go to the trouble of gathering leaves for us to hold, so that we [trade them] for a treat â to interact with us," he says.

Jojo also calls out when women he knows well visit his enclosure and then leave, making sounds that silverback males use to summon the females in the wild, explains Ãric Willaume, who runs the sanctuary.

When Jojo first arrived at the sanctuary after spending years in a small iron cage, he didn't look directly at people

Jojo is one of the more than 6,000 African primates estimated to have been bought or sold in the global trade of apes and other primates between 2000 and 2023. Thousands more are trafficked illegally for use as exotic pets, wild meat, and traditional medicine, the Pan African Sanctuary Alliance says., external

Experts believe when baby great apes are taken from their group at a young age like Jojo, they are left traumatised.

"If they're old enough to understand what happened to them, to understand their family was killed, they just lose the will to live," explains conservationist Tony King, from UK charity the Aspinall Foundation. "You see it in their eyes â the light goes out. I never knew where that expression came from, but I've seen it in gorillas."

King â who specialises in rehabilitating rescued great apes â remembers rescuing two infant sister gorillas who were brought to a sanctuary in the Congo Basin.

"They were clinging to each other," he says. "They stayed together in a little tight ball for about three days before they sort of unravelled. I think that's what kept them alive."

Sisters Likendze and Matoko clutch each other after they were brought in a wooden crate to the Project Protection des Gorilles sanctuary in the Congo

African charity the Gorilla Rehabilitation and Conservation Education (Grace) Center uses what it calls "gorilla-led" rehabilitation. When the team rescues a young gorilla from the pet trade, they put it with a group as soon as possible so they can socialise and essentially learn to be a gorilla.

But Jojo's case is unusual. He is fully mature and showing signs of harmful, chronic stress, according to Forrester.

In a game-changing approach to studying anxiety and trauma in apes, she has used a thermal camera to film Jojo's face. This allows her to monitor blood flow, revealing what appears to be a chronically cold nose.

It's a sign of a malfunctioning stress response. When any primate â a human, chimp or gorilla - is stressed, blood flow to the eyes and ears increases. It puts them on high alert for danger, but leaves their nose, temporarily, colder. For Jojo, this "nasal dip" as it is known, does not seem to recover.

"Being connected to others is incredibly important to our psychology as great apes," Forrester says. "Having another individual to share his life with would be so healing for him.

"I would assume other gorillas would help him process this grief and trauma."

Thermal imaging of Jojo's face reveals signs of chronic stress, with a persistently cold nose indicating a potentially disrupted stress response

Dr Katie Fawcett, from the Grace Center, says it's also "essential" for an animal to form social relationships to be considered for release into the wild - which is the ultimate goal for Jojo's keepers.

The bonds between gorillas â what we would call friendship - is key to their society, adds Dr Robin Morrison from the University of Zurich, who studies the social lives of mountain gorillas. In one recent study, she discovered that female mountain gorillas would seek out females they knew, even if they hadn't seen them for many years, when joining a group.

And she says a big part of a gorilla's normal social life is "just sitting together quietly sharing space".

But Jojo has never been given the opportunity to sit quietly with another gorilla.

No one knows how Jojo will react to a companion. Moving an animal from an accredited zoo would be a decision for an international committee and based on genetics, health and the animals' personalities and compatibility.

Any introduction to another animal would be done very carefully â perhaps giving them an object that smells of the other before they see each other.

These slow, cautious ape introductions do happen â when an animal moves from one zoo group to another, for example. Forrester has contacted a number of accredited zoos to explore the possibility of doing this for Jojo. But those discussions have fizzled out with no sign of progress.

The problem, she says, is "there aren't clear processes in place to do these kinds of trans-locations".

Encounters between two or more separate gorilla groups are complex social events

The international rules and processes for moving captive gorillas across international borders - from one zoo or safari park to another - are designed for breeding programmes.

"They're not designed for reintroductions into the wild, or to find companions for animals that might need them," says Forrester.

And because Jojo, who is probably in his 20s, has reached maturity, King thinks it might be too late for him to bond with other gorillas.

"Jojo's situation is unique, but he is probably best where he is," King says, adding that introducing him to another animal could, somewhat ironically, be a source of more stress for him.

Prof Forrester studies Jojo as part of research to understand how our understanding of human mental health - particularly stress and trauma - can be applied to our great ape relatives

Being alone is also natural, even relatively common, for mature male gorillas. According to Morrison, about a third of adult males are solitary. "When they reach adolescence, they tend to disperse and spend a good chunk of their life on their own."

Solo males will try to persuade females to form a group with them, but some don't succeed and may feel lonely as a consequence, Morrison believes. "If we think of loneliness in an evolutionary context - this mismatch between the social interactions they want and the social interactions they're getting. I suspect feeling lonely is part of the natural, evolutionary drive to attract females."

But while those phases â of family life and adult solitude - play a role in wild gorilla society, Jojo was forced into solitary confinement as an infant.

Morrison believes Jojo is probably both traumatised and lonely.

"[In the wild] gorillas move together, find food together, sleep together," she says. "So much of their lives is just being with this group."

That is what Jojo's keepers, Forrester, and others want for Jojo. A friend. Maybe a female, or a compatible male.

"It would be good for him to have a companion," says keeper Makaba. "Because being alone is like being cut off from the world - it's like being in prison.

"Having another gorilla there would be a good thing for him."

#69. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#67. Katherine Deves among four NSW upper house candidates revealed by One Nation

Party’s NSW leader Mike Newman says contenders are ‘people from the real world’ and not ‘career politicians’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The failed federal Liberal candidate Katherine Deves, who campaigned to exclude transgender women from women’s sport, will stand for One Nation at next year’s New South Wales election.

Deves was among four NSW Legislative Council candidates announced by One Nation’s NSW leader, Mike Newman, on Sunday.

The other candidates were Arnon Wither, a construction worker from the Hunter region who works as an electorate officer for Senator Sean Bell; Manti Steele, a farmer, teacher and mother of eight from Dubbo; and Daniel Hill, a surf lifesaver from the state’s south coast.

The NSW election will be held on 13 March.

Newman said on Sunday the upper house candidates were “authentic”, “people from the real world” and not “career politicians”.

One Nation has only announced a single lower house candidate – Newman, in the electorate of Cessnock – but he said the party would run candidates in all 93 lower house seats.

“We want to make sure that these people bleed orange, and like I said, they’re all from the real world,” he said.

“That’s the that’s the type of person we’re aiming at … people from the real world who understand business, who understand their pinch points and pain points, understand the cost of living, understand cashflow.”

Deves was handpicked by the former prime minister Scott Morrison to run in the federal NSW seat of Warringah in 2022. Her candidacy was marred by controversy, including when she likened anti-trans activism to standing up against the Holocaust.

On Sunday she said she joined One Nation “because I wanted to be a part of the rescue, recovery, and restoration of our nation”.

“We are seeing cost of living skyrocket, whether it’s fuel, housing, energy, and we are watching a government that is so wedded to destructive ideologies like DEI and net zero that they are prepared to impoverish us all,” she said.

If elected, she would look to make changes to state laws regarding “discrimination, hate speech, vilification, birth certificates, etc”.

“Where we can restore clear and accurate language around biological sex, men and women, and ensure that women and girls are safe and have spaces for services, sports, etc, where it’s inappropriate for males to be there.”

One Nation has required candidates at state elections to sign non-disclosure agreements preventing them from discussing their potential candidacy with the media.

News24 reported last month that Deves would run as an upper house candidate in the NSW election. She announced that she joined One Nation in September.

When Guardian Australia contacted Deves about her potential candidacy in October, she directed inquiries to Vikki Campion, the NSW party’s part-time media adviser. The party provided a statement saying it had confirmed only three candidates at that time – Newman, the lead upper house candidate, Stuart Bonds, and a Tamworth firearms manufacturer, Jeff Bacon.

#65. Is Italy the Model for Europe’s Future?

Over more than a quarter-century, I’ve watched Europe’s politics become more like Italy’s, with all the resulting instability, as the far right came in from the cold.

Jason Horowitz, previously bureau chief in Rome, is now bureau chief in Madrid. This article is adapted from his recent book, “The Italian Job: A Memoir of an American Reporter in Rome.”

When I first moved to Rome, I spent my time living in the past.

At the turn of the century, I drifted through a vintage postcard of postwar Italy: old women sitting around tables they had dragged into the street, old men playing cards in caps, cats arching their backs on the hoods of snub-nosed Fiats.

As newspapers filled with talk of Silvio Berlusconi, a media mogul with ambitions and appetites worthy of a Roman emperor, my meanderings inevitably took me toward Tiber Island, a sliver of land in the middle of the river that divides Rome.

An ancient bridge on the island’s left bank had a strange statue of weather-beaten faces positioned back-to-back as if they couldn’t stand one another. The heads rose out of the shared torso of a square pillar and represented Janus, Rome’s god of the past and future, the deity of transitions and change.

Back then, I didn’t see the change around me, but now, more than a quarter-century later, after spending decades reporting on Italian politics and changes across Europe, I have come to understand that I was living in the future.

In the time since, I have seen an Italianization of politics all around the continent, with coalitions of small parties resulting in instability, with fed-up voters desperate for new saviors. But more than anything, I have seen — in Britain, France, Germany — countries following the Italian model by allowing the far right into the political mainstream.

#64. How telecoms upgrade work sparked US flight chaos

Watch: US ground stop caused by issue during copper wire update, transport secretary says

An upgrade to a decades-old telecoms network in the US caused the longest nationwide grounding of flights since the 11 September 2001 attacks.

Friday's stoppage occurred when part of a key system that allows air traffic controllers and pilots to communicate was switched to fibre from copper wiring so old it has been discontinued, said US Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy on Saturday.

US planes were grounded for more than four hours, affecting thousands of flights and passengers.

Earlier Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said there was no indication of a cyber-attack and that everybody was safe.

On Friday afternoon, a government contractor called L3Harris was continuing work on upgrading an aviation telecoms network.

As it swapped out copper lines for fibre at a remote location in the US, L3Harris installed a router - a piece of equipment which basically directs traffic across the network.

According to Duffy, that router started flooding others in the air traffic control system, sending 1,000 connection attempts every four to five seconds.

As a result, 17 out of 22 air route traffic control centres in the US experienced "instability" on voice and ground communications, said Bryan Bedford, administrator of the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA).

It took the aviation authority 45 minutes to identify where the problem was because maintenance is logged manually on paper.

This meant the FAA had to "pore through" the physical documents "to figure out who's working on what", Bedford admitted.

As a precaution as it tried to track down the cause, Bedford said the decision was made to ground all flights.

Aviation analyst Miles O'Brien described the US system as "a house that's about to be condemned".

He told the BBC: "I think most of us could look at this and go 'this is a system in the United States that we have allowed to decay'. It is worse than a fixer-upper."

Duffy said the system needed a complete overhaul, estimating a $50bn price tag.

In the meantime, he said authorities would work to determine whether it was "incompetence" or faulty equipment that led to the outage.

He also said there would be "re-evaluation" of allowing upgrades to be done during the day.

In a statement on Saturday afternoon, L3Harris confirmed it was working with the FAA to modernise the airspace communications infrastructure and said it was supporting the agency with its investigation.

"L3Harris takes the nationwide ground stop and its impact on travelers, airlines and air traffic operations seriously," the firm said.

Juliette Kayyem, US assistant secretary of Homeland Security under President Barack Obama, questioned why an upgrade was done on one of the week's busiest days for travel.

"You don't do it on a Friday when there's lots of air traffic controllers," she said

"You try to find ways to minimise its upgrade so you're not maybe doing it across the board in a single moment but you also have to have systems that are ready to step in if the upgrade in any way begins to threaten the system," Kayyem added.

A spokesperson for L3Harris said is it "supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the 13 November ground stop of our nation's air travel".

He added: "L3Harris takes the nationwide ground stop and its impact on travellers, airlines and air traffic operations seriously."

The ground stop led to more than 8,500 delayed and nearly 8,000 cancelled flights on Friday, according to FlightAware.

The FAA's initial announcement at 15:54 EST (20:54 GMT) said all flights departing within the US would be grounded until further notice.

More than 3,500 airplanes were in the air at the time. Some were diverted to other airports and others flew on to their destinations.

Hours later, some flights were allowed to take off from San Francisco International airport.

Have you been affected by the flight disruption? Get in touch

'We'll get home one day' - US travellers impacted by nationwide ground stop

All US-bound air traffic from Eurocontrol member states was suspended.

There are 42 Eurocontrol member states, including dozens of EU nations, the UK and Turkey, as well as Israel and Morocco.

Passengers travelling from the UK have been warned of possible disruption on Saturday morning.

A number of flights to the US from Heathrow airport were cancelled and delayed, but Manchester airport said no significant disruption was expected. Flights were operating as normal from Gatwick airport.

Australia's largest international airport, Sydney, had grounded all US-bound flights, but scheduled flights are now expected to depart as planned.

Air Canada also cancelled flights between the US and Canada.

Duffy said on Saturday: "We are back at full operations."

But, as he predicted on Friday, it is taking time to unwind what happened.

FlightAware said more than 5,900 flights were cancelled and more than 7,000 delayed in the US on Saturday.

Delta Air Lines and United Airlines said they were resuming operations but they both cancelled 20% of their flights on Saturday. American Airlines cancelled 520 journeys.

Meanwhile, JetBlue cancelled 40% of its scheduled journeys.

By Sunday morning, the situation had improved, but the impacts of the stoppage were still being felt with more than 2,400 flights delayed and 1,400 cancelled.

Luggage from cancelled flights at Ronald Reagan airport

In the US, UK and the EU, airlines may be liable for compensation if the reason for a delay is deemed within their control, such as a fault with the plane or pilot sickness.

As such, airlines do not have to provide compensation to passengers for air traffic control problems.

But passengers are still protected to a certain degree under US federal - and country-specific - laws.

Under the FAA Reauthorization Act of 2024, cancelled or "significantly delayed flights" - defined as three hours for domestic flights and six hours for international routes - must be offered alternative travel or a full refund of the fare if the passenger chooses not to fly.

It also requires all air carriers to issue refunds for tickets purchased with credit cards within seven business days of the request, and 20 days for tickets purchased with another form of payment. Passengers accepting a new flight cannot receive a refund.

In the US, there is no requirement for airlines to provide meal vouchers or accommodation, but some may offer complimentary support to customers.

However, if passengers are travelling from the UK to the US or flying with a UK carrier, you may have additional rights.

Under UK law, airlines have a duty to look after you when flights are delayed or cancelled for whatever reason.

a reasonable amount of food and drink (often through vouchers)

a way for you to communicate (often by refunding the cost of calls)

free accommodation, if you have to stay overnight to fly the next day

If your airline is unable to arrange assistance, you have the right to organise this yourself and claim back the cost later.

The Civil Aviation Authority advises people to keep receipts and not spend more than necessary.

It was only the second such nationwide stoppage since the immediate aftermath of the al-Qaeda plane hijackings of 11 September 2001.

"This looks like the worst since 9/11, but clearly not on that scale," Dr Guy Gratton, an associate professor of aviation at Cranfield University, told BBC Verify.

Gratton pointed to a US grounding in 2023 due to a failure in the Notice to Air Missions (Notam) system.

"That lasted 1 hour and 52 minutes, delayed about 9,500 flights and cancelled about 1,300," he said.

The 2023 outage meant air traffic safety notices could no longer be published and flights were grounded until that system was restored.

Have you been affected by the US flight disruption? Tell us your story.

#62. US probes nationwide flights stoppage as travel disruptions persist

US air travel is expected to return to normal this Sunday, following a nationwide ground stop Friday night that stranded hundreds of thousands of passengers and disrupted travel worldwide. The ground stop now raises questions about the country's aging infrastructure and lawmakers are calling for an investigation. Analysis by Philip Mann, aviation expert at Vector Strategic Consulting.

#61. Anthony Albanese apologises for hot-mic gaffe after Coalition criticises ‘locker-room talk’

Prime minister says he and Chris Bowen were having ‘light-hearted banter’ when they were caught discussing where they keep their phones

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

Anthony Albanese has apologised for a hot-mic gaffe at the UN climate talks in Turkey, saying he regretted being overheard discussing mobile phone radiation and “dicks” in a candid conversation.

“I of course regret that. I apologise,” Albanese said during a visit to the Australian warship HMAS Stalwart in the Philippines on Sunday.

“This was a private conversation, we thought, between two friends having a bit of light-hearted banter.”

Ahead of a meeting at the Cop31 climate summit in Antalya on Friday, Albanese and the climate change minister, Chris Bowen, were caught on camera discussing which pocket they use to keep their mobile phone.

Albanese can be heard telling Bowen that he should store his phone away from his heart because “it can interfere with your rhythm”. Laughing, Bowen asks whether the advice was because a phone can be “a little bit radioactive”.

Albanese says “it makes absolute sense”, before Bowen replies that he was more conscious of putting his phone in his pants pocket. The prime minister asks: “Because of your dick?”

Bowen agrees and says: “Arguably more important than the heart.”

On Sunday, Albanese played down the incident which occurred ahead of a meeting with business leaders in Antalya.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

“No one was hurt or harmed. We weren’t having private banter which criticised anyone,” he said.

The speedy apology is in contrast to previous gaffes by Albanese, including long-running controversy over comments about the gift of melons from Japan’s prime minister, Sanae Takaichi, on a podcast.

Albanese previously apologised for saying he would “shag” Australian singer Kylie Minogue in the same episode of the Bush Deep podcast.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

The melons comment risked becoming an international incident, as Albanese repeatedly insisted the comments and his corresponding hand gestures were not a reference to breasts.

Japan’s government sent a diplomatic cable to Australia in July, days after the podcast originally aired, claiming there had been “sensationalised reporting in the Australian media” and saying they detected no “ill intention” in Albanese’s statement.

The Coalition on Sunday criticised Albanese and Bowen’s comments.

Opposition frontbencher James Paterson said the remarks were unfortunate, telling News24 they could damage impressions of Australia on the world stage.

“That locker-room talk like that has a time and a place and it’s not at international summits when you’re the prime minister of Australia talking to your energy minister,” he said.

But the employment minister, Amanda Rishworth, dismissed the criticism, saying the comments were a joke.

“This is a private conversation in between meetings,” she said.

“I know that a hot-mic incident is very exciting for people ... but really, there are bigger issues to tackle and discuss.”

Earlier a spokesperson for the prime minister said the hot-mic comments were “a joke in between meetings”.

Albanese is in the Philippines for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit and high-level diplomatic meetings, including with the US president, Donald Trump.

He will hold formal bilateral talks with the Filipino president, Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr, on Sunday and visited a Jollibee fast food restaurant to celebrate the chain’s expansion to Australia.

#59. Saudi-Backed Forces Retreat in Yemen, Leaving Key Strait in Houthi Control

It’s a setback for the Yemeni government and its ally Saudi Arabia, as analysts said the momentum in country’s civil war was tilting toward the Iran-backed Houthis.

Forces backed by Saudi Arabia in Yemen said on Sunday that they were withdrawing from the country’s Red Sea coast, where they have been fighting for weeks to retake strategic territory from the Iran-aligned Houthi militia.

A group of fighters called the Southern Armed Forces, who are allied with the government of Yemen, said in a statement that they were “redeploying” to protect the interim capital, Aden, citing recent battlefield developments and “threats targeting” the city.

Aden has been the seat of the internationally recognized government for more than a decade, since the Houthis seized control of Sanaa, the Yemeni capital.

The retreat is a blow for the Yemeni government and Saudi Arabia as the balance of power in Yemen continues to tilt toward the Houthis, analysts said.

“The Houthis are very determined to go to Aden,” said Ahmed Nagi, a senior analyst for Yemen at the International Crisis Group. “They don’t want to see any area controlled by the government or the Saudi side, so they want to take over everything at this moment.”

#58. My mother told me I would die if I sought treatment for Ebola - I am proof you can survive

When Dr Raymond Kasereka Kamwanga told his family he suspected he had Ebola and was going to a treatment centre in the city of Butembo in eastern Democratic Republic of Congo, his family were horrified.

They regarded the specialised unit as a "death house" as not many patients do recover - especially those admitted in the late stages of the illness. His 50-year-old mother was in tears, pleading with him not to go there.

"Why are you choosing to die at the Ebola treatment centre?" Masika Ndungo had asked him.

But as a doctor at Butembo's Katwa Hospital, he knew that if stayed at home, he was unlikely to survive.

This was in July, two months after the World Health Organization (WHO) had declared the Ebola outbreak in DR Congo a public health emergency of international concern. Since then 5,400 people have died and 11,000 have been infected, making it the second-most deadly outbreak ever recorded.

Butembo is in North Kivu province, the epicentre of the crisis, with the highest number of reported cases, according to the Congolese health ministry.

Dr Kamwanga is one of 3,031 people who have survived - and is evangelical about sharing his story in an effort to help stop the spread of infections devastating his community.

After staring death in the face and overcoming ostracisation, he has even started writing a book about his experience.

"As someone who has recovered, I am living proof that the disease exists, but also that it is possible to recover from it," he tells the BBC outside Katwa Hospital where he still works.

The 30-year-old medic believes he contracted the virus through contact with a patient in a setting where protective measures are not always adequate.

Raymond Kasereka Kamwanga uses his book to help convince sceptics that Ebola is real

The first symptoms appeared gradually: a bitter taste in his mouth, headaches and a general feeling of malaise. He contacted the Ebola response team.

After an initial consultation and some tests, he returned home, but his condition did not improve.

Butembo does not have the equipment to quickly test for Ebola - samples need to be sent to the city of Beni, 54km (33 miles) north.

This delays diagnosis as the condition of roads in this war-torn and underdeveloped part of DR Congo means it can take between 48 and 72 hours to travel even this relatively short distance.

After three days at home, Kamwanga felt extremely weak and experienced muscle pain. On the fourth day, he began vomiting and had diarrhoea.

This is when he gathered his family members - including his mother - to tell them he had to go to the Ebola treatment centre.

"She had started crying, along with my sisters. I told them everything would be alright, that I was a doctor and that I was confident. But they weren't reassured," he says.

Around 18:00, he set off on foot to the nearby centre but refused to use the main entrance.

He called his colleagues and asked them to put on their protective gear before they approached him and isolated him. The virus spreads from one person to another by contact with infected bodily fluids.

Dr Kamwanga's mother says his experience has helped dispel some of the misinformation she had heard about Ebola

The current outbreak is caused by the Bundibugyo species of the virus for which there is no approved vaccine or treatment.

"The treatment given to Ebola patients is symptomatic - meaning that doctors treat the symptoms the patient presents," says Kamwanga, explaining that in his case it was severe vomiting and diarrhoea.

In some patients these symptoms can lead to organ failure and then some develop internal and external bleeding.

Kamwanga lost a lot of fluid and struggled to stay hydrated - he was terrified.

"I was afraid for myself. Every day, patients were dying right before my eyes. But I stayed positive," he explains.

Over 13 days lying in his hospital bed, he tried to keep his spirits up by watching movies and TV shows on his phone, reading and scrolling through TikTok.

The day he learned he had recovered, Kamwanga felt immense relief. He thanked the healthcare workers who had cared for him.

However, even a certificate showing he was Ebola-free did not initially dispel the unease those around him felt after he was discharged.

What is Ebola and why is stopping the latest outbreak so difficult?

'I buried my parents one day after the other' - Ebola mourners learn how to grieve safely

"We welcomed him home warmly. But because of our fear, we didn't get close to him any more," Ndungo, his mother, tells the BBC.

For 21 days - the incubation period of the virus - the doctor lived separately in another part of the family home, which was crowded at the time with at least 18 people living there, including several children and people displaced by recent fighting in the region.

He would only see them from afar when he went to pick up his meal, while other family members kept their distance.

Neighbour Kavugho Sifa shared these concerns.

"We were really afraid, but we prayed to God to protect him at the treatment centre. With God's help and the doctors' efforts, we saw him return in good health," she tells the BBC.

They all agree the experience changed their perception of Ebola.

Some do not believe the disease even exists - Ndungo says that witnessing Ebola first-hand helped to dispel some of that misinformation.

After his recovery, Kamwanga threw himself into community outreach - sharing his story as a way of combatting rumours and mistrust.

"I started writing my book - The Price of Saving Lives - one week after recovering and leaving the hospital," he says. "However, it is not finished yet. What I currently use during my awareness-raising activities is the draft, and it is already making a significant impact."

During these sessions, he also distributes photos taken while he was sick to help convince residents that Ebola is real.

But in Butembo, this mission remains difficult. Kamwanga explains that some people view awareness campaigns as a way for humanitarian organisations to profit from donor funding.

Such mistrust, which is also levelled at healthcare professionals, is often fuelled by poverty and trauma related to years of armed conflict, he says.

"Ebola has come alongside the war," he says, explaining that some even interpret it as a way for those in authority to threaten a vulnerable population "to finish off those whom the war failed to exterminate".

He also believes that the number of deaths observed during epidemics - compared to recoveries, which are sometimes less visible - reinforce fear and suspicion.

But putting himself on the front line also comes with risks - and Kamwanga says he receives death threats on his phone.

Some residents accuse him of being paid to spread information about Ebola. He also reports that outreach workers have been attacked by members of the community.

He feels the authorities do not work effectively to address all these problems: "Our government is taking things lightly."

He agrees too with the recent report by MÃ©decins Sans FrontiÃ¨res (MSF) that Ebola funds are going missing.

"The money allocated to the Ebola response is being misappropriated by the government. We do not have enough protective equipment," the medic says.

The BBC has asked the Congolese government for comment.

Despite the intimidation and the obstacles he and other medics face, he remains determined - his goal is to help put an end to the disease.

"Should we give up the fight against Ebola? No. We continue to do what we can with the resources available to us, which are unfortunately very limited."
