#57. Anthony Albanese welcomes Trump-Xi-Putin summit – even as European leaders express reservations

‘We think dialogue is a good thing,’ Australian PM says after German chancellor urges the US to work with Europe for peace in Ukraine

Australia has endorsed this week’s historic summit between Donald Trump, Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin in China, even as European leaders warn that the US president must not make major concessions over the war in Ukraine.

The three-way summit is planned to take place in Shenzhen, where the leaders will gather for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) meeting. The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, has warned the talks must not lead to a redistribution of influence among world powers or the abandonment of his country, more than four years into the war sparked by Putin’s full-scale invasion.

Speaking in Manila on Sunday, the Australian prime minister, Anthony Albanese, welcomed the talks taking place but insisted the clear message to Putin was that the war should end.

Albanese, in the Philippines before a meeting of Quad leaders and talks with south-east Asian leaders at the Asean summit, left open the possibility of joining an Apec group photo with Putin if China wished for it to go ahead.

“We think that dialogue is a good thing and we await the outcome of that dialogue,” he said.

“It’s obviously quite significant, the fact that President Trump will be at Asean as well as at Apec, and that we’ll have a Quad leaders meeting as well.

“We want to see something constructive come out of that, including we want to see a de-escalation in the conflicts around the world.”

German’s chancellor, Friedrich Merz, expressed hesitation about the talks at a meeting of EU leaders on Friday.

“I want to state our message to the US very clearly once again: please, no surprises,” he said. “Stay on Team Europe and Team Ukraine, and let us work together for a genuine peace in Ukraine.”

France’s president, Emmanuel Macron, warned against forcing “an unjust peace” on Ukraine and, by extension, all of Europe.

“That is the spirit of defeat,” Macron said. “That is a recipe for another war.”

The meeting has been compared to a 1945 summit at Yalta, in southern Crimea, when then US president, Franklin D Roosevelt, Britain’s Winston Churchill and the Soviet leader Joseph Stalin carved up influence across war-ravaged Europe. Zelenskyy has warned that the meeting must not turn into “Yalta 2, 3, 4”.

The historian Timothy Garton Ash has called the trilateral summit, organised after a proposal by Putin last month, “a sort of parody” of Yalta, even as analysts say decisions on the future of Ukraine or China’s ambitions to take control of Taiwan without their involvement is unlikely.

Albanese said the summit would not undercut a growing push by countries including Australia and Canada for middle powers to have greater influence in world affairs.

“We’ll continue to engage with like-minded countries,” he said. “There are two super powers in the world.

“What’s important about the struggle of the people of Ukraine is that they’re not just standing up for their own sovereignty; they’re standing up for the international rule of law.”

Asked if he would join the so-called “family photo” of Apec leaders if Putin took part, Albanese said he would consider the wishes of China as the host nation. But he said the Russian president should know Australia’s opposition to the invasion.

“If he’s in a room where I am, I’m sure they’ll hear my contribution,” Albanese said.

After the G20 foreign ministers’ meeting, Australia’s Penny Wong joined with European and Canadian counterparts to avoid a group photo. It was cancelled by US officials when a third of participants declined to stand alongside the Russian foreign minister, Sergei Lavrov.

Albanese signed a new defence agreement with the Philippines on Sunday, visiting the supply ship HMAS Stalwart in the Port of Manila. Australia is helping to strengthen the Philippines’ defensive capability against Chinese aggression.

More freedom-of-navigation naval exercises are planned in an attempt to counter Beijing’s efforts to influence the South and East China seas.

#55. Judo champion shakes up French presidential race with surprise candidacy

Five-times Olympic champion Teddy Riner is one of France's most popular athletes

Apart from an overwhelming urge to pin one's opponent to the floor of the house, there is no obvious connection between politics and the world of top-level judo.

That has not stopped France's much-garlanded world heavyweight champion Teddy Riner from throwing his hat into the ring for next April's presidential race.

The Guadeloupe-born 37-year-old may have no experience of public office, no party behind him, and as yet no manifesto. All so far is a slogan La France qu'on aime (The France we love), a declaration on social media, and a campaign visit to a hospital in the Seine-Saint-Denis suburb of northern Paris.

But it would be a foolhardy pundit who would totally write off his chances.

Riner is, after all, one of the most popular figures in modern-day France, regularly topping the polls for embodying the nation's "values". He is a proven winner, with five Olympic golds and 11 world championships under his belt, as well as a successful career in business.

More than 28 million people have watched his election broadcast on Instagram and X, shot with proper presidential decorum against a backdrop of books and a French flag. Some 22% told an Ifop poll within 24 hours they "could" vote for him.

Teddy Riner's video has attracted 14.5 million views on Instagram alone

Actors and television personalities have become presidents in other countries. The ex-leader of Liberia â George Weah â was formerly a professional footballer.

So why not a judoka in the ÃlysÃ©e Palace?

"Anyone who thinks that being out of the political in-crowd disqualifies Riner from running for the ÃlysÃ©e would be very wrong," says Guillaume Tabard of Le Figaro.

"But [Riner] too would be wrong if he thinks not belonging to the in-crowd is all it takes."

For now the whole of the French political world has decided to take the Riner candidacy seriously â or at least to pretend to. That is partly because he is such a popular personality that mocking his political hopes could easily rebound badly.

But it is also because the state of French politics â outside of the burgeoning forces of the hard left and hard right â is one of total disarray.

The centre-ground is floundering, with no candidate yet emerging who can inspire anything like the sense of mission of hard-right leader Marine Le Pen and Jean-Luc MÃ©lenchon on the hard left, who for now look set to face off in the final round of the presidential election in May.

So when a popular figure like Riner enters the race - proclaiming himself "neither left, nor right", wanting to "unite the country" and "put money back in people's pockets", railing equally against "racists" and "yobs who scare people off our streets" â politicians have to take note.

Hard-right leader Marine Le Pen currently leads the polls in the race to be France's next president

It is perfectly possible, probable even, that Riner's campaign will fizzle out after a brief period of hype, and that the judoka will after all be a joker in the pack. But it is also possible that it won't, in which case the pre-election arithmetic will need a hasty readjustment: and it won't be the centrists who are smiling.

Thus far, Riner has shown himself a master of the codes required for this kind of out-of-nowhere candidacy, the first of which is to press home the message: "Don't Worry! I'm NOT a Politician!"

In this day and age in France, anyone who smacks of the "system" is automatically downgraded in voters' eyes, so it is important to emphasise that you are only running for office because, one, you have experienced real life (unlike those loafers in Paris); and two, you have discovered that there are solutions out there for the country's many problems. Solutions that are invisible to those same blinkered apparatchiks.

All this Riner was able to express in last Monday's social media broadcast by using sophisticated rhetorical devices.

"People say I am a fool for wanting to run for the presidency," he said.

"Am I a fool? Well, if refusing to see France sink under the weight of debt; refusing to see mothers and fathers grit their teeth every time they do their weekly shop, and tremble with fear when their kids go out on the streetâ¦ if all that means being a fool, then yes call me a fool."

It was a promising oratorial start for the sportsman, and certainly attracted the viewers.

Are student protests being manipulated ahead of France's presidential election?

France's Marine Le Pen, fighting court conviction and running for presidency

But if he is serious â and Riner says he never does anything half-heartedly - that's not how he became an Olympic champion â then the hard work begins now.

What does Riner actually want to do? Who will be on his team? Where does he position himself on the all-consuming matters of the day: pension-reform, investment in education, the cost of living, public debt, the rise of populism or Russia's war in Ukraine?

In short, how does he intend to transform his undeniable popularity as a sporting figure into a political mandate?

Protesters have for weeks taken to the streets in France over wages, the cost of living and public services

Broadly, Riner appears to be on the right on economic matters and law and order, but being black and of Caribbean background he is also very strong on racism. When he says he is anti-extremism, he is referring mainly to Marine Le Pen.

In other words his pitch is somewhere in the centre, and against the extremes.

The trouble is, this is already an extremely crowded area, with important figures like ex-prime ministers Edouard Philippe and Gabriel Attal signally failing to take off.

Currently there are a dozen or so candidates in the area between centre right and centre left, stretching from the Socialist-backed RaphaÃ«l Glucksman to the Republicans' Bruno Retailleau on the right.

The latest possible addition is Christine Lagarde, who steps down as head of the European Central Bank next February.

President Emmanuel Macron is among the few politicians who appears genuinely pleased by Riner's decision to run

The addition of yet another contender will dilute their polling numbers even further, making a second-round qualification for MÃ©lenchon more likely.

So no wonder the leader of France Unbowed (LFI) seemed so happy about Riner's decision to run. "We know how it goes: neither right nor left means not left," MÃ©lenchon told reporters on Friday.

The only other politician who seems genuinely pleased, rather than putting on a fixed smile, is President Emmanuel Macron, who said Riner was a "great champion" and that politics "did not belong to the professionals".

That could be explained by the president's cool relations with his former prime ministers, Philippe and Attal, both of whom are doing all they can to distance themselves from their unpopular one-time boss.

Riner said he viewed Macron's praise as a "poisoned chalice", telling reporters on Saturday that he was "not the president's envoy, I'm not a Macronist".

Politics and judo have very little in common, but of the two, as Riner will soon discover, the more vicious is not the one that is fought out hand-to-hand after mutual acknowledgement on the tatami mat.

In Japanese, judo actually means "the gentle way".

If he starts looking like a serious candidate, Riner must know his life will be turned upside down, not just his physical form.

In politics you do not bow in respect to your opponent. You give him a metaphorical headbutt.

#53. Watch: Divers explore US ship sunk off Florida coast to create new reef

Activists unsuccessfully fought for the historic liner - which crossed the Atlantic Ocean hundreds of times - to be preserved as a museum.

#51. Gender-diverse Australian sailors barred from US submarines despite Aukus deal, documents reveal

Defence briefing released under freedom of information laws shows ADF beholden to US directives when in training

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Aukus is effectively forcing the Australian navy to comply with a US ban on transgender people in the military with gender-diverse navy recruits prevented from starting a career in nuclear submarines because they would not be safe training on US boats.

Internal Australian defence force documents show transgender and non-binary sailors and officers are explicitly banned from Australia’s nuclear submarine training program because of US policies.

An internal defence briefing, released under freedom of information laws, said while ADF policy on equality and inclusion was unchanged, “US government policy is different”.

“ADF personnel undertaking duty in the US (including training) are required to comply with US policy,” the briefing said.

“Consequently, transgender and nonbinary ADF personnel and ADF career applicants are currently unable to commence the SSN [nuclear-powered attack submarines] training pathway.”

Australia, without a nuclear submarine fleet of its own, has hundreds of sailors and officers training on US nuclear submarines and on US bases.

At a women in defence event in October, Rear Adm Tish van Stralen was asked how the defence force was “managing the recruitment of gender-diverse and LGBTQ+ Australians into the nuclear submarine pathway” given the US directive.

Van Stralen, one of the highest-ranking women in the Australian navy, said the ADF was a diverse and equal opportunity employer.

But she said potential recruits for the nuclear submarine program were told the ADF was “subject to policies and laws in other countries, so we have to be very mindful”.

“We also have an obligation, a duty of care to our people, so we do not want to put our people into any sort of harm’s way or anything that might adversely affect them.

“I’m working very closely with navy at the moment and with the submarine agency around the training opportunities because it would be very difficult for me to put a gender-diverse person into that training at the moment.”

Van Stralen said the navy would seek alternative career paths for gender-diverse recruits prohibited from the nuclear submarine program. She said the UK, Australia’s other Aukus partner, had “more liberal policies” and that training opportunities “may arise in the UK”.

“We don’t want to disadvantage people because of it but we are subject to the laws and policies of the US when we are in their country.”

The US defence secretary, Pete Hegseth, last year issued a directive to his department “to eliminate all diversity, equity and inclusion offices and initiatives”. He also has sought to ban transgender personnel from joining the US military – repeatedly using offensive slurs in public statements – and ordered that transgender personnel be purged from the services.

Donald Trump signed an executive order in January 2025 – the first week of his second term as president – which banned transgender service personnel and said gender-diverse people were unfit to serve.

Announcing military cuts in September amid continued attacks on diversity, Hegseth declared that the US military would accept “no fatties, no trannies, no beardos, no weirdos, no wimps, no radicals”.

Under the Aukus deal, it is proposed that Australia will buy three Virginia-class nuclear submarines from the US, beginning in 2032. In the lead-up to that, Australian sailors are training onboard US Virginia-class submarines and at US bases, while US-flagged nuclear submarines are porting in Australia.

Crewing Australia’s proposed nuclear submarine fleet is likely to prove one of the Aukus agreement’s most critical, sustained challenges. Virginia-class submarines have a complement of 135, more than double the crew for Australia’s diesel-electric Collins-class submarines.

Last week Australia’s defence minister, Richard Marles, said the Aukus timeline presented the country with a significant “training challenge”.

“We just need to be training more people and that’s what we’re doing,” Marles said at HMAS Stirling, the West Australian port through which US nuclear-powered submarines have been rotating.

The Australian defence department did not respond to questions from the Guardian about the impact of adhering to the US policy directive, including how many Australian service personnel have been prevented from undertaking nuclear submarine training.

The government’s nuclear-powered submarine workforce strategy urges defence to “proactively expand the talent pool through active inclusion of underrepresented groups, including women, First Nations Australians, regional communities, and individuals who may not traditionally see themselves in technical or defence-related careers”.

#47. Australia, UK, Canada and Italy urge Israel to allow critical aid to Gaza ahead of winter

Four countries call situation ‘entirely reversible’ in joint statement as flooding exacerbates humanitarian crisis

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

Australia, Canada, the United Kingdom and Italy have urged Israel to allow critical humanitarian aid to enter Gaza, describing the situation on the ground as “dire”.

The four countries released a joint statement on Monday morning, urging Israel to allow 150,000 tents and other vital supplies being held at the border into Gaza ahead of winter.

“We, the Foreign Ministers of Australia, Canada, Italy and the United Kingdom, are clear that as winter takes hold in Gaza, the humanitarian situation on the ground is dire,” the statement said.

“The recent floods have brought further suffering – and this was only the first storm of the winter. People in Gaza desperately need shelter and humanitarian assistance.”

The ministers said Israel has time to act “before the winter fully sets in”.

“This situation is entirely reversible … we today urge the government of Israel to stop the delays now and allow immediate, rapid and unimpeded humanitarian access throughout Gaza,” the foreign ministers said.

“The crossings must be opened, and the tents allowed in. Dual-use items necessary for humanitarian operations and early recovery must be granted entry before it is too late.”

Tens of thousands of Palestinians in Gaza have been left in waterlogged and damaged tents after deadly storms in the past week. Thousands more have been left without shelter while sanitation infrastructure has also been destroyed by storms.

At least four Palestinian children and woman were killed as heavy rains and wind battered fragile, leaky tents.

Australia’s Greens leader and foreign affairs spokesperson, David Shoebridge, said “harsh words” from the government would do little, and that the government should instead take more concrete action.

“The Israeli Government has not responded to harsh words from Australia before, they won’t now. Material action, sanctions on the war cabinet, ending the two-way arms trade, implementing sanctions on illegal settlements as other nations have done. These are real actions the Albanese Government can take but has failed to,” Shoebridge said.

Shoebridge said Australia previously refused to join commitments by multiple allies including the UK that called for import bans on trade from illegal Israeli settlements.

“When it was a statement committing to material action, Australia disappeared,” Shoebridge said.

The foreign ministers urged all parties to implement the comprehensive plan for Gaza, respect the ceasefire and “their obligations under international humanitarian law, and allowing aid to reach the people who need it.”

On Monday, Australian cabinet minister Madeleine King told ABC radio the four nations had worked on the statement.

“This particular group has been working on this kind of statement for a little while to make sure that people that are affected by the war in the Middle East can receive the assistance they need,” she said.

“But, really importantly … is the fact that this humanitarian aid will be able to be delivered.”

#46. 'Can you believe it?': SpaceX catches part of its Starship rocket on return to Earth

SpaceX has successfully caught the top part of its huge Starship rocket on its return to Earth for the first time.

"Can you believe it?," a commentator said as large crowds cheered at the launch site in Texas.

The company had hoped to catch both the bottom and upper stages of the rocket using mechanical arms - nicknamed chopsticks - on its launch tower.

But the first catch of the rocket's booster was aborted a few minutes after the launch on Friday. The booster was diverted to the ocean, bursting into flames just above the sea.

The top part, known as the Ship, returned to Earth a few minutes behind schedule at around 15:20 local time (21:20 GMT) on Saturday, in the first-ever successful catch attempt.

#45. Iran Attacks Bahrain for the Second Time in Days

The resumption of attacks after a two-month lull comes as peace talks between the U.S. and Iran appear to have stalled.

Vivian Nereim reported from Riyadh, Saudi Arabia, and Aaron Boxerman from Jerusalem.

Iran launched a drone attack against Bahrain on Sunday for the second time in days, as peace talks with the United States have faltered.

The Bahraini Defense Ministry said the Gulf Arab country’s armed forces had intercepted several “Iranian aerial threats” on Sunday before they struck their targets. Bahrain’s Interior Ministry also issued two alerts to residents on Sunday morning advising them to seek shelter.

There were no immediate reports of injuries or damage. Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps claimed that Iranian forces had targeted a radar system at a Bahraini air base.

Bahrain separately hosts a major U.S. naval base. The American military did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Sunday’s attack came two days after the U.S. military said it had shot down two drones fired by Iran toward Bahrain, without causing any injuries or damage.

The Revolutionary Guards said that Friday’s attack was retaliation for a previous American strike on a telecommunications tower in Iran.

The attacks were the first to target Bahrain in about two months. After the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran in February, Iran launched thousands of retaliatory missile and drone attacks at Gulf Arab countries that host American military installations. But those attacks had largely petered out as the two sides entered a stalemate.

#42. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#41. Ronaldinho makes comeback in Ravenna win

Ronaldinho played the first 45 minutes for Ravenna against Forli

Brazil legend Ronaldinho made his comeback aged 46 for Ravenna in their 2-0 win over Forli in an Italian third-tier derby.

The 46-year-old came out of retirement for the Serie C club he joined as an investor in August, having last played in 2015 for Fluminense.

He started Sunday's game and showed some bright touches, and he was involved in the opening goal.

The former Barcelona superstar held off a defender before playing the pass that came before the assist for Giulio Donati's drive from outside the box.

He had earlier set up a chance for Donati from a corner, with the defender having his header saved.

At half-time there was a media circus around Ronaldinho, with journalists surrounding him, and former Brazil and AC Milan team-mate Dida joined him on the pitch.

Ronaldinho was substituted at the break with the score at 1-0.

A temporary stand, named the R10 in reference to Ronaldinho and his familiar shirt number, was built for the sold-out fixture at the Stadio Bruno Benelli.

The game was streamed in several countries around the world.

Ronaldinho trained once with the team, on Saturday, before Sunday's debut.

A team graphic beforehand showed him on the left side, but he mostly played centrally.

In his peak playing years, Ronaldinho was one of the best players in the world - as well as one of the most popular for his joyful demeanour and exuberant skill.

He won the 2002 World Cup with Brazil, the 2006 Champions League with Barcelona, and league titles with Barca and AC Milan - plus the Ballon d'Or and Fifa World Player of the Year awards in 2005.

He also played for Paris St-Germain as well as Gremio, Flamengo, Atletico Mineiro and Fluminense in his homeland, plus Mexican side Queretaro.

Ronaldinho scored 299 goals in his regular career and had been hoping to have a chance for number 300 for Ravenna. He would have taken a penalty had they been awarded one.

"It was lovely to be here, playing, having the build-up in the dressing room," Ronaldinho told Sky Sport Italia.

And asked if he might play again for Ravenna, who are second in their Serie C group, he said: "If the president calls me, I'll be back."

All of Ravenna's players - and not just the starting XI - posed with Ronaldinho in the team photo before kick-off

#40. Nvidia scandal: US treasury chief blames Lutnick’s commerce department over alleged lack of action

US commerce secretary faces sack as questions emerge over senior Trump officials’ knowledge of alleged Chinese spy’s ties to Jensen Huang

The US commerce secretary, Howard Lutnick, is facing growing pressure over his apparent lack of action over the Nvidia spying scandal as other Trump administration officials publicly raise questions over his decisions.

Scott Bessent, the US treasury secretary, blamed Lutnick’s commerce department for the fact the US had not imposed export restrictions on certain Nvidia chips to China.

Jensen Huang resigned from Nvidia on Thursday, and was replaced on an interim basis by the company’s chief financial officer, Colette Kress, after reports emerged of his multiple affairs, most importantly with Alice Huang (no relation), who is suspected of being a Chinese spy.

White House officials are discussing whether Lutnick should be fired, the Associated Press reported, after it emerged that a US intelligence report about Huang’s relationship with the alleged spy was circulated among senior figures in the Trump administration.

Lutnick is now scheduled to testify before Congress next month, just days after the ousted Nvidia chief answers lawmakers’ questions on Capitol Hill.

Lutnick is also facing criticism for a delay by the commerce department in adding Singapore-based Megaspeed, which is associated with Alice Huang and has bought billions of dollars of Nvidia chips in recent years, to Washington’s blacklist of companies that the US considers a risk to national security despite reported concerns within the administration.

Bessent has “for months” been in favor of putting Megaspeed on the blacklist – but Lutnick’s commerce department “has the pen”, Bessent told CNBC. “You will have to ask them about their internal process,” he added in an interview with the network on Friday.

John Curtis, a Utah Republican senator, said that Lutnick’s reported delay on export controls regarding Megaspeed “raises the question of whether the commerce department is acting in the best interests of the American people”.

The Republican senator John Cornyn told the Financial Times that there needed to be “a thorough investigation and accountability for the outrageous circumvention of national security export controls”, adding: “Heads should roll.”

The Senate Democratic leader, Chuck Schumer, said Jensen Huang and Nvidia must answer as to whether US security had been jeopardised, adding that the Trump administration also must account for what it did once it learned about his reported relationship with Alice Huang.

“Secretary Lutnick has proactively agreed to appear voluntarily before the banking committee to address their questions,” Kristen Eichamer, a spokesperson for Lutnick, told NBC News.

Trump’s close ties with Jensen Huang have also come under renewed scrutiny as a timeline of the saga started to emerge. The president called him two days before he stepped down and urged him to fight for his job, the New York Times has reported.

On Thursday, Schumer questioned what Trump knew when he personally invited Jensen Huang aboard Air Force One on a state visit to Beijing earlier this year, adding: “Americans deserve to know that those at the highest levels of power are putting America first, not their bottom line or their personal friendships.”

The House Democratic leader, Hakeem Jeffries, said he had demanded a classified briefing for all members of Congress about Nvidia.

Republican Tim Scott, the Senate banking committee chair, said he had sent letters asking Lutnick and Jensen Huang to appear before him, citing “recent reports [that] raise serious ‌concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology”.

The statement from Nvidia’s board of directors confirming Huang’s departure did not mention the spying scandal and personal-conduct revelations, instead paying tribute to his “profound” contributions to the company he cofounded.

Huang is reported to have had relationships with Alice Huang as well as an Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, according to the New York Times, and another, unnamed employee, according to Reuters.

The House foreign affairs chair, Brian Mast, a Republican, said on Thursday that congressional staff had spoken to Nvidia on Wednesday night about a date and location for Huang’s testimony.

The House speaker, Mike Johnson, said on Thursday there was now growing consensus in Congress on limiting chip exports to China.

Nvidia’s advanced AI chips are widely used to develop cutting-edge AI models, making the company important to the US race against ​China to build superior AI systems. The US has sought for years to ‌limit China’s access to Nvidia’s chips because it does ‌not want the Chinese military to benefit from them.

“The export controls on chips to China has been a big issue around here,” Johnson told reporters ‌at the US Capitol, adding that he favoured limiting China’s access to the advanced technology. “I think there’s a developing consensus in Congress that is an important position to take.“

Chinese AI companies are now trying to secure Nvidia chip capacity outside China, amid growing concern that the gap in US export controls may now be closed after the scandal, people familiar with the matter told Reuters.

Beijing has also stepped up efforts to develop domestic alternatives, including through Huawei, although analysts say Chinese chips remain less efficient for training advanced AI models.

Lawmakers are pressing their colleagues to pass at least four bills on chip exports, according to statements issued since Monday.

On Chinese social media, many expressed surprise at what they see as Huang’s swift resignation, and mourned the sudden end of an era that lasted for more than three decades.

News of his resignation attracted more than 400m posts overnight on the Chinese microblogging platform Weibo.

Huang had enjoyed rock-star status in China, where his visits drew crowds seeking selfies and autographs and his casual outings became social-media sensations. During a January 2026 visit, opportunistic sellers advertised photographs with him for 25,000 yuan ($3,700), while a Beijing restaurant launched a special menu costing more than 400 yuan after he ate there.

#39. Judge blocks Trump closure of 'Trash Island' Washington DC golf course on Sunday

A federal judge has temporarily blocked the US government's closure of an historic golf course and park in Washington DC.

US District Judge Ana Reyes has been overseeing a months-long challenge to the president's plans for East Potomac Golf Links, which he said would be closed from 15 November for extensive renovations.

Reyes has said President Donald Trump cannot close the park nor its golf course, but added that his administration was not required to actively manage either.

Trump, an avid golfer, has said his plans to upgrade the 106-year-old course will create "one of the Best and Most Spectacular Golf Courses anywhere in the World". The BBC has contacted the White House for comment.

Declaring the current course "Trash Island", he announced in October that it would be renovated over 15 months to create a space that "will be open to the Public while, at the same time, designed to the Highest Level of Golf and able to hold World Class Tournaments" - despite the ongoing litigation.

Reyes said in a 30-page decision issued on Saturday that the Trump administration's justifications for the proposed course closures were pretextual and that the government had violated at least two federal laws "to meet an arbitrary deadline".

"The government proposes to close a historic park, daily enjoyed by Washingtonians of all stripes, indefinitely," Reyes wrote.

She said the closure "runs counter to the public interest in utilising national parkland", adding that the government had not "plausibly identified any harm it would suffer from a temporary restraining order that preserves the status quo pending further proceedings".

'Trash Island' or DC landmark? How a golf course got Trump in a messy legal dispute

A coalition of advocacy and legal groups opposing the closure and renovation said they are "grateful that the court has stopped the government from unlawfully closing East Potomac Park and depriving people of this priceless piece of American history and wonderful recreation space".

"Our laws prevent this administration from acting without a fair and public process, and today's order will stop the government from ignoring those laws," said the coalition, which represents the plaintiffs and includes Democracy Forward and the Democracy Defenders Fund. "We are eager to continue to argue this case in court."

The legal dispute dates back to February, when the DC Preservation League and others sued over redevelopment plans for the park.

The historic course, which sits two miles (3km) south of the White House, is one of three public courses run by the National Park Service (NPS) in the nation's capital.

The legal fight then escalated in October when the NPS began to cut down hundreds of trees without notifying Reyes until 15 hours before work was set to begin.

After the trees were cut, Reyes ordered the Trump administration to stop tree removal until at least 26 October and give her three business days' notice about making any other permanent changes.

Despite the work stoppage, Trump announced that the course would be closed for 15 months from 15 November to accommodate for the renovations. He also shared a rendering of the course redesign.

"The current Course is old, broken, dilapidated and of no further use or effect. It is in very poor condition, with falling, dangerous and disease-infested trees and, importantly, a collapsing and hazardous seawall," he said in a post on Truth Social.

Reyes has since separately opened an inquiry into whether justice department attorneys had "made misrepresentations to the court and otherwise violated ethical rules".

That included whether giving late notice to the court about the renovation plans had been "done in bad faith", and whether the attorneys failed to correct previously false statements or withheld information.

She said she would carry out the inquiry with a special master, an independent third party.

Judge investigating whether Trump administration lied about golf course renovations

#37. Philippines’ Carmelle wins first Eurovision contest in Asia

Filipino entry, pop and rap song Sobra-Sobra, takes 19-year-old to victory at grand final in Bangkok

Carmelle Collado has won the first Eurovision song contest Asia, taking the Philippines’ entry, Sobra-Sobra, to victory in Bangkok on Saturday night, at the first regional edition of the contest to be staged outside Europe.

Eleven countries competed at the One Bangkok Forum in central Bangkok, and the show ran for two and a half hours including advertising.

The winner, who performs under the single name Carmelle, finished on 211 points, 41 clear of the hosts Thailand, with Vietnam third. She received the most votes from both the national juries and the public ballot.

Carmelle, 19, grew up beside the railway tracks in the Bicol region of south-eastern Luzon and has been competing in televised talent contests since she was 12.

Sobra-Sobra mixes pop and rap, is supported by traditional Philippine instruments, the kubing and kulintang, and is sung in Tagalog and English.

After her victory, Carmelle said her song celebrated female empowerment and was intended as “a tribute to all Filipino women”.

“I made history, since this was the first time,” she told the Associated Press. “It’s such a big responsibility for me, because this one is a global stage, so thank you everybody who voted for me.”

“It’s about how unapologetic, independent and strong Filipinas are,” she told the Guardian before the final. “It describes me – not just me, but of course all the Filipinas.”

The show opened with a routine celebrating the host country, in which Muay Thai fighters shared the stage with drag performers on street food carts. The 11 acts were then driven on in tuk-tuks, each carrying their national flag.

Vietnam’s Tóc Tiên, a star at home for more than 20 years, performed first, among dancers costumed as lotus leaves. Mang x Daou represented Thailand, a duo assembled for the contest in the host country who performed a duet staged like a musical number.

Nepal’s Pratik Himal Rai gave one of the most conventionally Eurovision performances of the night with Ananta Maya, a rock ballad staged with fire imagery and a large heart-shaped structure behind him.

He ended it by appealing for support for his country, which he said was “going [through a] very hard situation”. More than 1,500 people were killed and more than 5,000 were reported missing after a rock and ice avalanche near the Chinese border triggered catastrophic flash floods in August.

Bangladesh’s Beatmosphere, originally a nine-member a cappella and beatbox group, sang without a single instrument. Three of their members were left out of the final due to a rule capping each act at six performers on stage.

Nyendra from Bhutan sang dressed in the gho, his country’s national dress for men, against a backdrop of Himalayan peaks. Mongolia’s Beck performed in a reinterpreted deel, also a national costume.

Half the result was decided before the show began. The 11 national juries voted at a dress rehearsal on Friday night and put the Philippines at the top with 93 points, ahead of Vietnam and Malaysia.

The other half came from viewers around the world, who could vote online but not for their own country, and who were given eight free votes each and the option to buy 10 more.

Laos’s entrant, Thinla, was unable to hear her own music during her performance, and her dress rehearsal was aired once all live performances were over. She finished fifth, with the night’s third-highest public score.

Carmelle receives no prize money but does get a trophy presented by Dara, the Bulgarian singer who won this year’s European contest, and will perform at Eurovision 2027 in Burgas.

Organisers have said the right to stage future editions will not automatically go to the winner, on the grounds that smaller countries lack the infrastructure.

Some of the clearest echoes of Eurovision’s camp traditions came through those who announced the votes.

South Korea’s jury points were read by Jo Kwon, a veteran pop star who has played Adam in the Korean production of Priscilla, Queen of the Desert and the title role in Everybody’s Talking About Jamie.

The Philippines’ were read by Vice Ganda, a gay comedian who is one of the biggest stars on Philippine television.

Meanwhile, the guest performer was the Thai singer Jeff Satur, who reached a wider Asian audience through KinnPorsche, a 2022 series in the genre of male same-sex romance dramas that has a large following across Asia.

Organisers intend the Bangkok show to be the first of many regional editions, with the executive producer, Assaf Blecher, saying that 16 or 17 territories had expressed interest in competing next year, among them China, Hong Kong and Taiwan.

#36. Iran targets US military base in Bahrain with drone attack

Iran and the US are vying for control of the Strait of Hormuz

Iran launched a drone attack against a US military base in Bahrain on Sunday, its second attempt to target the country in three days.

The country's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) said it targeted the US air defence radar facility at al-Riffa air base in Bahrain using six drones.

Bahrain's military said its air defence systems intercepted and destroyed the Iranian air attacks "before they could achieve their objectives".

It comes after Iran fired drones at Bahrain on Friday, its first attack against Gulf countries since September.

The US and Iran are locked in a stand-off over the Strait of Hormuz. About a fifth of the world's gas and oil supply usually flows through it but shipping has been restricted since the outbreak of war in February.

Iran insists that it should control the strait and has been attacking what it calls "unauthorised" traffic, while the US military is providing protection for tankers shuttling oil through the strait to keep exports flowing from Gulf Arab states.

As sirens sounded across Bahrain on Sunday the interior ministry called on citizens to head to their nearest safe location.

Bahrain's foreign ministry said: "The Kingdom of Bahrain condemns in the strongest terms the continued brutal Iranian attacks on its territory and holds Iran fully responsible for their consequences."

In a statement on the strikes shared by Iran's state broadcaster on Telegram, the IRGC said: "The Strait of Hormuz is closed, and any aggression by the American enemy will be met with an even harsher and more forceful response."

Earlier this week, the IRGC claimed that it struck a US early warning radar system in Bahrain on Friday.

The US military has denied the claim, stating that the IRGC launched two one-way attack drones towards Bahrain and both were shot down by US forces. It added that there were no injuries or damage.

Earlier in the week the US carried out strikes on Iran's Qeshm Island in the Strait of Hormuz, US officials told CBS, the BBC's US partner.

The officials described the strikes as limited and defensive, and said they were in response to the IRGC firing on a US aircraft.

Iranian state news agency Irna reported explosions on Qeshm on Monday. The IRGC did not comment but said its forces had shot down two US MQM-170 drones south of the island and a US MQ-9 drone east of the Strait.

The US and Iran reached a preliminary agreement to end the war and reopen the strait in June, but it collapsed within weeks after Iranian attacks on shipping resumed and the US reinstated a naval blockade of Iranian ports.

Iranian negotiators had been discussing with mediators a proposal to reopen the Strait of Hormuz within two days if the US meets seven conditions, reportedly including the lifting of the naval blockade and releasing frozen Iranian assets.

However this week Donald Trump said that the US was no longer interested in a deal to end the war with Iran and ordered Vice-President JD Vance, who had been involved in efforts to restart negotiations, to focus on domestic US politics.

#35. Jordan Sentences 3 to Death Over Greek Orthodox Church Attack

The Jordanian military said that the men, who were arrested after a shooting at a Greek Orthodox church last month, had been convicted of terrorism charges.

A military-run court in Jordan sentenced three men to death on Sunday after they were convicted in connection with an attack at a church last month that killed nine people, the Jordanian Armed Forces said in a statement.

The shooting at a Greek Orthodox church, which also wounded about three dozen people, was one of the deadliest militant attacks in the country in years, and King Abdullah II vowed that it would be met with “the harshest retaliation.”

The three men were sentenced after being convicted of violating antiterrorism laws, according to the Jordanian military. They were found guilty of carrying out deadly terrorist acts, possessing weapons and ammunition with the intent to commit terrorism, and promoting the ideas of a terrorist group.

That group was not named in the armed forces statement.

The sentences are subject to appeal, the military statement noted.

The three men were arrested in late October in Jordan after a shootout with security forces during a predawn raid on their hide-out in an abandoned house in the southern province of Karak, where the church attack took place. Karak is home to a sizable Christian community.

King Abdullah praised the arrests on social media. “No proponent of dark ideologies that divide Jordanians will be tolerated,” he wrote.

Jordanian officials have not said what motivated the shooting at the church. Shortly afterward, Prime Minister Jafar Hassan said it was “the result of terrorist takfiri ideology,” using an Arabic phrase that often describes Islamist extremists who declare other Muslims heretics.

The sentencing on Sunday came a weeks after Jordan retaliated for the church attack with deadly airstrikes on Islamic State targets in southern Syria, a Jordanian government spokesman said at the time. The Jordanian military said it had been “targeting hide-outs of terrorist organizations,” without naming any specific group.

The airstrikes in Syria killed at least seven civilians, including three children, according to the Syrian civil defense rescue service, and the attack was condemned by Syrian officials.

Muslims make up more than 97 percent of the population in Jordan, and Christians make up about 2 percent, according to United States government estimates. The country’s Constitution declares Islam the state religion but safeguards the free exercise of religion and bars discrimination on religious grounds. Christian Jordanians are guaranteed seats in Parliament and have long served as cabinet ministers and ambassadors.

The Christian presence in Karak dates back more than 1,500 years, and there are multiple churches there to this day. Jordan also has important biblical and Christian pilgrimage sites, including one along the Jordan River where Jesus was said to have been baptized.

In June, Jordan hanged six men convicted of terrorism-related or drug-trafficking charges. The executions came after a nine-year hiatus in executions and were condemned by the European Union and human rights groups, including the Death Penalty Information Center and Human Rights Watch.

Ephrat Livni is a Times reporter covering news around the world. She is based in New York.

#34. World leaders head to Asia for back-to-back diplomatic summits

Tussle for influence as trade, tech and regional security to take centre stage in China, Philippines and Cambodia meetings

World leaders will fly in to Asia for back-to-back high-level summits this week against the backdrop of the tussle for influence between the superpowers China and the US.

Beijing will have the home advantage when it welcomes Donald Trump to its southern technology hub of Shenzhen for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) leaders’ meeting. It starts two weeks after the US leader lost midterm elections.

The summit also comes after an awkward-at-best revelation that the head of Nvidia, the US chipmaker that for a long time was the world’s most valuable company, was having an affair with a woman US intelligence suspects was a Chinese spy.

Apec represents more than 60% of global GDP and has primarily promoted free trade and multilateralism. This year’s meeting could be defined, however, by the confirmed trilateral meeting between Trump, the Chinese president, Xi Jinping, and Vladimir Putin of Russia.

The three-way meeting has been likened to a modern-day Yalta, when in 1945 then US president, Franklin D Roosevelt, Britain’s Winston Churchill and the Soviet leader Joseph Stalin met in Crimea to carve up Europe after the second world war. However, analysts say a “Yalta 2.0” that issues edicts on Ukraine and Taiwan without their involvement is unlikely.

Xiaoyu Pu, an associate professor at the University of Nevada’s department of political science, said a three-way meeting “might be more symbolic than substantive, although symbolism itself matters in great-power diplomacy.

“Trump appears interested in nuclear arms control and denuclearisation. Xi’s priority is likely to maintain a stable relationship with Washington while preserving China’s strategic partnership with Russia. Putin, meanwhile, wants to maintain his bargaining leverage over Ukraine.”

Apec begins on Wednesday and follows a flurry of other diplomatic gatherings, chief among them the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (Asean) meeting in the Philippines, starting on Sunday, which Trump will attend.

While he is in Manila, US officials will hold a sidelines meeting of the so-called Quad – Australia, India, Japan and the US. In addition, a Francophone summit, which includes the Canadian prime minister, Mark Carney, and the French president, Emmanuel Macron, is happening concurrently in Cambodia.

Critical global issues at all these summits will include AI supply chains and governance, tariff reductions and the green energy transition. Trump’s focus has been on denuclearisation. A key regional focus will be on the South China Sea, where Beijing has built massive military outposts in disputed waters, as well as the gigantic Indonesian forest fire haze blanketing the region.

The humanitarian and security crisis in Myanmar has become a perennial issue, as is status of Taiwan, which China does not recognise. The self-ruled island is sending the founder of the tech hardware company Acer as its envoy to Apec.

For well over a decade, Washington’s engagement in the region has been coloured by concern over the rise of China, with the then president Barack Obama telling Australia’s parliament in 2011 that the US was “a Pacific power, and we are here to stay” – a policy known as the “pivot to Asia”.

The strategy of intense diplomacy and trade deals was adopted by Joe Biden, who hoped to gather allies on the continent who wanted to balance their relationships with both superpowers.

Some analysts see Trump’s transactional style in Asia as a continuation of the pivot policy. Still, the president’s volatility and unpredictability have spooked smaller nations, who regard China as a more predictable partner.

Jayant Menon, an economist at Singapore’s Iseas-Yusof Ishak Institute, said that countries in south-east Asia had been “laying the groundwork for a future with reduced dependence on the United States” by seeking deals elsewhere, including with each other and the EU.

“Amid persistent uncertainty and an administration determined to levy tariffs by any legal means, south-east Asia’s ability to ride out another round of tariffs depends on deeper regional integration and diversification beyond the US market,” he wrote this month in the East Asia Forum.

Beijing’s more predictable policies do not necessarily make it any more palatable for its regional neighbours, however, and the Chinese premier, Li Qiang, could have tough talks when he goes to Manila for the Asean summit, especially on the South China Sea.

China’s top diplomat, Wang Yi, recently urged Manila to rein in Filipino politicians who he said had repeatedly “smeared” Beijing, after public outcries in the country over the disputed waters.

Brunei, China, Malaysia, the Philippines, Taiwan and Vietnam have been locked in decades-long territorial standoffs in the busy waterway, which could sit on deposits of oil and gas. Washington has sent military ships on “freedom of navigation operations”, despite Chinese outcries, although US-linked crises in Iran, Ukraine, Venezuela, and Palestine and Israel have sucked up Washington’s focus under Trump’s second term.

A decades-long attempt to sign a South China Sea pact is not expected to happen at this year’s Asean summit, as previously hoped. Despite monthly meetings, negotiations will have to continue next year, when Asean’s leadership rotates to Singapore.

Diplomats at the Asean summit will also be focusing on Myanmar, which is seeking reintegration. Each country in the group is assessing how to deal with their isolated neighbour. The timing of summits this week coincides with a judgment in the international court of justice on Thursday that could find Myanmar responsible for genocide. The coup leader, Min Aung Hlaing, remains barred from Asean but is allowed to send a representative.

Asia’s week of diplomacy will be followed next month by the G20 summit in Miami, Florida, which Xi is expected to attend if US and Chinese relations continue to hold steady.
