#32. Thousands of US flights still cancelled or delayed two days after grounding of planes nationwide

'We'll get home one day' - US travellers impacted by nationwide ground stop

Thousands of US flights were cancelled or delayed on Sunday as the US air-traffic control system continued to recover from a technical failure that led to a rare national ground stop.

Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy said normal operations may not be restored until Monday.

"We have airplanes and crews that are out of position, so it takes a little bit of time," he told the BBC's US partner CBS. More than 5,000 flights were delayed and 2,000 cancelled on Sunday.

President Donald Trump urged Congress on Sunday to approve more money to modernise the air-traffic control system. The grounding of all aircraft scheduled to depart the US on Friday caused chaos at airports across the country.

More than 3,500 airplanes were in the air when the FAA announced at 15:54 EST (20:54 GMT) that flights would be grounded until further notice. Some were diverted to other airports and others flew on to their destinations.

In the aftermath, 8,500 flights were delayed and another 8,000 cancelled, according to FlightAware.

The stoppage left passengers stuck inside planes on the tarmac, unable to take off or to return to their gates. Others were stranded in airports, sleeping on the floor and desperately trying to get in touch with their airline. The ground stop also had a knock-on effect on schedules around the world, with some flights out of the UK and Europe cancelled.

The ground stop was lifted after about four and a half hours, but the impacts continued over the weekend.

On Saturday, airlines cancelled more than 5,900 flights and more than 7,000 others were delayed.

Investigators are still working to determine the root cause and who is at fault, but Duffy said the administration decided to ground flights after failures in a key system that allows air traffic controllers and pilots to communicate.

The transport secretary said a computer router installed as part of an upgrade from copper wire to fibre started sending out faulty signals.

He said on Sunday that the router - a piece of equipment which directs traffic across the network - was installed in El Dorado, Arkansas, and "flooded our network".

Duffy said that issues will continue to arise until the US makes major modernising changes to its air control system. The system needs a complete overhaul, he said.

"Let's not lie to each other," he said on NBC's Meet The Press. "We have to make investments into a network that has been neglected for decades."

In a post on Truth Social on Sunday, Trump urged US lawmakers to allocate the money, saying "we can get it built quickly and efficiently".

"It will be the BEST SYSTEM anywhere in the World. Much of our current air control system is 50 years old - RIDICULOUS! We have already started the work, but it should be done all at once, not in drips and drabs," he said.

Experts say the US uses antiquated technology for flight communications.

Aviation analyst Miles O'Brien described the US system as "a house that's about to be condemned".

He told the BBC: "I think most of us could look at this and go 'this is a system in the United States that we have allowed to decay'. It is worse than a fixer-upper."

#30. 160 questions, only one wrong: top scorer in South Korea tax exam admits AI cheating

Fears for national college entrance exam next week as test taker admits sending photos of questions to AI tool

The marks attained by the top scorer ⁠on South Korea’s national exam for aspiring tax accountants this year seemed too good to be true. On Sunday, sceptics were ⁠proven right when he ⁠confessed to ​using generative AI to cheat.

The scandal has followed months of speculation after he scored near-perfect marks on hundreds of questions over two exams ⁠taken in April and July. It has also raised the stakes for organisers of South Korea’s annual college entrance exam this Thursday.

“I cheated using ⁠electronic devices and generative AI. My passing the exam with the top score was not ​the result of fair effort,” the candidate said, ‌identifying himself by the ‌surname Han, as he apologised on the YouTube channel Attorney Lee Don-ho.

Han, who ranked first in both rounds of the qualification exam held in April and July, confessed through his lawyer that he used AI to obtain answers during the tests. He said he had cheated by ‌taking photos of the exam questions with a microcamera, sending them to an AI tool and checking the answers on a smartphone.

His score of 397.5 out of 400 points in the first round, with only one wrong answer out of 160 questions, had sparked accusations that he cheated using smart glasses, which he initially denied.

Speaking on camera wearing a face mask, Han said he had submitted a written confession to Seoul police.

The Seoul metropolitan police agency said: “We received a written confession through the test taker’s lawyer this morning regarding cheating on the tax accountant exam,” adding that the case had been assigned to investigators.

The scandal has rattled a ‌country where standardised tests largely dictate social mobility and career trajectories. It comes just days before Thursday’s annual national college entrance exam, ‌a high-pressure event for which many South ​Korean students will have spent much of their lives preparing.

“The presidential office views ​the matter very seriously, and because the college entrance exam is next week, we have been working hard to prepare countermeasures,” a senior presidential office official told reporters on Sunday.

The official said reports of potential cheating in the certified public accountant exam were concerning, adding ⁠that the presidential chief of staff for policy would convene an emergency meeting later ​in the afternoon to ​address the issue.

The prime minister, Han Seong-sook, acknowledged on Friday public concerns over AI-assisted cheating. The government would “prepare thoroughly in advance and closely oversee the process until the very end to ensure the college entrance exam is conducted fairly and stably”, a statement from her office said.

Reuters and Agence France-Presse contributed to this article

#29. Clothing factories shut in Bangladesh as workers strike over pay

Protesters in Dhaka, Bangladesh, on Friday demanding a new minimum wage

Dozens of factories in Bangladesh's main clothes-producing area have been shut by workers striking over a higher minimum wage.

Nearly 150 sites across the country have been closed in the textile hubs of Savar, Ashulia and Gazipur this weekend.

The garment industry is big business for Bangladesh, generating nearly $40bn (Â£30bn) in exports every year and making up 10% of the country's total GDP.

Workers are striking over demands for a minimum wage of 30,000 taka (Â£182) a month. The minimum monthly wage in Bangladesh is currently 12,500 taka but employees say this is not enough to deal with the cost of living crisis.

On Sunday morning workers massed on the Dhaka-Tangail highway in Gazipur to block the road. Traffic was stopped by the protests for about an hour before police stepped in.

A "No Work No Pay" provision of labour laws allows owners to suspend operations and withhold wages when production is disrupted.

Police Supt Mohammad Amjad Hossain told the BBC officers shut factories in the city after the workers walked out.

He said 13 factories were closed on Saturday night and "sixteen factories were closed during the morning unrest".

Wage protests force hundreds of Bangladesh garment factories to close

The garment industry in Bangladesh employs about four million workers making clothes for big brands including H&M, Primark and Zara.

Bangladesh is the world's second largest exporter of ready- to-wear clothes after China and it is feared that the current unrest could have a knock-on effect for international buyers if it carries on for substantial amount of time.

The Bangladesh Garment Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BGMEA) and the Bangladesh Knitwear Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BKMEA) is calling for protection for factories.

"BGMEA and BKMEA have questioned the justification of continuing to participate in wage board negotiations without any assurance that the factories will be protected," BGMEA President Mahmud Hasan Khan said.

"Garment factories that have been unable to continue production due to the ongoing unrest will remain indefinitely closed from tomorrow under... Bangladesh's labour law."

On 1 November the government formed a minimum wage board to recommend a revised pay structure for workers.

The board, chaired by a senior district judge, said at its first meeting it would finalise any recommendations by the end of November.

Once it is put in place, the revised pay structure is expected to take effect from 1 December.

#27. Argentinian lawyer mocks UK’s sovereignty claim in Falklands oil case

Lawyer says Britain wrong to believe in historic doctrine that crown has sovereignty over parts of oceans

Britain should not be permitted to act as a 17th-century colonial power treating the waters around the Falkland Islands as a Dominium maris Britannici, Argentina has told an international tribunal.

Marcelo Kohen, representing Argentina at a hearing in Hamburg, mocked Britain for apparently believing in the historic doctrine that the British crown has sovereignty over expansive parts of the oceans.

Argentina has applied to the international tribunal for the law of the sea (Itlos) for a ruling that would force Britain to suspend the drilling for oil in the North Basin of the Falkland Islands ahead of further arbitration.

The Sea Lion oilfield is about 130 miles north of the Falkland Islands, a British overseas territory over which Argentina has a longstanding sovereignty claim.

Argentina’s president, Javier Milei, reportedly shared among his cabinet on Tuesday a photograph of the Argentinian legal team in Hamburg, describing it as “tremendous”, adding that “we are working on the Falklands issue like no one else in history”.

Addressing the tribunal on the second day of hearings, Kohen said that the dispute over the archipelago of 700 islands in the south Atlantic – known in Argentina as the Malvinas – did not mean that the area was a “legal black hole” and that the UK could do as it wished with its natural resources.

“The fact that the parties dispute sovereignty does not turn the areas surrounding the Malvinas into some kind of Dominium maris Britannici,” he said. “We are not in the 17th century … We are in an era where we should put an end to the last vestiges of colonialism.”

Ellie Reeves, the attorney general for England and Wales, told the tribunal’s panel of judges on Tuesday that Argentina was seeking to further its sovereignty ambitions by applying for suspension of the drilling.

She claimed that such a move would kill the Sea Lion project, with “disastrous consequences” for the Falkland Islanders and the companies involved. The UK claimed that the tribunal, which adjudicates over maritime matters, does not have jurisdiction to wade into matters of sovereignty.

Kohen, switching from French to English, said the UK had failed to mention in its submissions the “elephant in the room” or the “whale in the bathtub”. The UN regarded the Falkland Islands as a non-self-governing territory, he said, and had called in 1976 for both sides to refrain from taking decisions that would unilaterally change the situation before the sovereignty dispute was settled.

The exploitation of hydrocarbons would put the islands among the “highest ranks of wealth just below the principality of Monaco” while depriving Argentina of its rights to those resources, Kohen told the tribunal.

Argentina’s legal team claimed that suspension of the drilling, which is due to start in March next year and which could double the GDP of the islands, was urgent given the irreparable damage to Argentina’s rights.

In his closing remarks to the panel of 22 judges, Mario Oyarzábal, a legal adviser to Argentina’s ministry of foreign affairs, said they rejected the UK’s attempt to characterise the proceedings as an attempt to litigate the sovereignty dispute.

He said: “The question before this tribunal is a much narrower one: whether, while the underlying dispute remains unresolved, the UK may unilaterally authorise or carry out new drilling for the exploitation of hydrocarbons in the disputed maritime area.”

Sam Wordsworth KC, responding for the UK, said there was nothing in the convention on the law of the seas, over which Itlos has jurisdiction, which obliged a state not to “aggravate” a sovereignty dispute and that the UN resolution cited by Kohen as the “elephant in the room” was not legally binding.

The UK further claims there is no need for urgent suspension of drilling as oil extraction would not take place until 2028, before which an arbitration panel would have come to a conclusion on Argentina’s wider claim that its rights have been breached.

During his recent speech at the UN general assembly, Milei described the reclaiming of the Falklands as a “national cause”. Argentina invaded the islands in 1982 on the orders of the dictator Gen Leopoldo Galtieri. The 74-day conflict ended with the surrender of the Argentinian forces and cost the lives of 649 of its military personnel, 255 British military personnel and three Falkland Islanders.

Itlos is expected to make its ruling on Argentina’s case on 28 November.

#26. Three men sentenced to death in Jordan over fatal church mass shooting

Nine people were killed in the shooting at the Greek Orthodox church last month

A Jordanian court has sentenced three men to death by hanging for carrying out a fatal mass shooting on a church last month.

The attack on 23 October killed nine people and injured 34 others who had been attending a Christian funeral at a Greek Orthodox church in Karak governorate.

Jordan's Armed Forces said in a statement on Sunday that the men had been convicted of committing "terrorist acts", as well as possessing weapons, "promoting the ideas of a terrorist organisation" and "conspiracy to commit terrorist acts".

The three men - two Jordanian brothers and their brother-in-law - fled the scene of the attack and were arrested three days later. They have not been publicly identified.

Karak is home to a sizeable Christian community in the Muslim-majority kingdom.

CCTV footage from the day of the attack showed three men holding guns approaching and firing on St George's church in the village of Hmoud.

Watch: CCTV footage appears to show gunmen near church

A second video showed dead and wounded people lying on the ground amid chaotic scenes at the church.

It is still unclear why the attackers chose to target that specific church.

Shortly after the attack, Jordan's King Abdullah II wrote on X: "The terrorist attack against Karak will be met with the harshest retaliation.

"Those who delude themselves into thinking that they can divide Jordanians with their bullets have no chance in the face of our unity."

The three men fled the scene of the shooting and were later located inside an abandoned house in Karak governorate, south of the capital, Amman.

They refused to surrender and opened fire on security forces, who returned fire and wounded all three of them, the Public Security Directorate said at the time. No security personnel were hurt in the shootout.

King Abdullah praised security forces for chasing the suspects "to the very end".

The week after the Karak shooting, Jordan struck what its military said were "terrorist organisations" in Syria in retaliation to the church attack.

Syrian health officials told state media that five people, including women, children and an elderly man, were killed in the strike on a village in the southern Daraa province. Jordan said its fighter jets had killed and wounded "several terrorists" without giving further details.

Jordan said it had co-ordinated with the Syrian government, but Syria said it had not been notified in advance.

Executions in Jordan are carried out by hanging. The kingdom previously observed an unofficial ban on executions from 2006 to 2014, and another pause from 2017 until June this year.

#24. In Banff, a Grizzly Bear Known as ‘the Boss’ Rules the National Park

Bear 122 has survived a train, cars and rival bears. Some fear the biggest threat to his life now is his own fame.

“The Boss,” the near mythical grizzly bear king of Banff National Park.Credit...All in the Wild Photography

Reporting from Banff National Park, Canmore and Dead Man’s Flats, Alberta.

To the scientists and wardens of Banff National Park he is grizzly bear 122. But to everyone who lives in or near Canada’s oldest national park, and many of its 4.5 million annual visitors, he is the Boss.

He has been struck by a freight train without apparent ill effect. He has shrugged off being hit by cars. Scientists have confirmed that he killed and snacked on a black bear at least once, and he is believed to have fathered many, if not most, of the bears in his 2,000-square-kilometer territory.

Most of all, the Boss has become Canada’s 700-pound celebrity bear king. When he strolls along the Trans-Canada Highway looking for dandelions, “bear jams” swiftly follow. His battles with a rival bear known as Split Lip are legendary. (He caused the split lip.) His annual awakening from hibernation, usually in March, is reported by news outlets across Alberta.

Galleries in Banff and neighboring Canmore ship giant photos of him worldwide. His image appears on fridge magnets, T-shirts and tote bags. A local restaurant is named for him, its main dining room dominated by a scrap metal sculpture of the bear.

But despite his fearsome demeanor, enhanced by four-inch claws, few bear experts consider the Boss a threat to park visitors or residents. Rather, they fear his celebrity and the legions of fans it have created, may be the biggest threat to his life and his dominance.

#23. Ex-member of radical Marxist group scales balcony, escapes house arrest in Argentina

Leonardo Bertulazzi, 74, awaiting extradition to Italy, reportedly used harness to flee custody

A 74-year-old former member of Italy’s radical Marxist group the Red Brigades has escaped from house arrest in Argentina, the country’s security ministry confirmed on Wednesday, after he reportedly hid his ankle monitor inside a tube of pork and lowered himself from a fifth-floor balcony.

Authorities placed Leonardo Bertulazzi under house arrest in Buenos Aires in 2024, prior to his extradition to Italy to serve a 27-year prison sentence. He had received the sentence in 1987 for joining a subversive association and belonging to an armed group.

After years on the run, Bertulazzi was first arrested by Argentine authorities in 2002. He was freed a few months later and secured refugee status in 2004, but was arrested again in 2024 after it was revoked.

On Wednesday, Argentina’s security ministry offered a $13,000 reward for information leading to Bertulazzi’s recapture after he slipped out of detention.

The fugitive held a senior position in the Red Brigades, a radical Marxist organization responsible for attacks from 1969 to 1980, dubbed Italy’s “Years of Lead”. The group most notoriously kidnapped and assassinated former prime minister Aldo Moro in 1978.

Security footage showed Bertulazzi dropping from a building into a public road as cars drove past.

A detective tracking the former member of the Red Brigades reportedly told La Nación that Bertulazzi rappelled down from the fifth floor using a harness.

“First, he removed his electronic ankle monitor and placed it in a tube filled with pork, along with a blood pressure monitor to maintain the temperature. He then climbed on to a roof and, using a rope, lowered himself five meters until he jumped onto a vacant lot behind the building where he lived, and then scaled a metal gate to reach the street,” the detective said, according to the publication.

The footage, which indicated the time was 6.01am on 10 November, showed a possibly shaken Bertulazzi sitting down to rest for a few moments.

Police noticed his ankle monitor had stopped transmitting movement data and headed to the house, where they found the device discarded and Bertulazzi nowhere to be found.

Argentina approved his extradition to Italy in July 2025 after the country’s supreme court rejected a final appeal.

#21. Police ban protests near 10 schools and five universities in Paris

Some 106,000 people from a wide range of groups took to the streets in France on Saturday, including 24,000 in Paris

Police in Paris have issued a five-day ban on protests near 10 secondary schools and five universities as authorities try to control weeks of blockades and violence at educational venues in France.

The order prohibits unauthorised gatherings or marches within 100m of the sites from 16 to 20 November.

Paris police chief Patrice Faure said the ban was necessary due to "numerous disruptions and blockades around certain secondary schools and establishments of higher learning" and "persistent violence".

A wave of protests began in France September over poor secondary school conditions, before spreading to universities, workers and a "yellow-vest" movement against the cost of living.

Announcing the order, the Paris police chief said blockades around certain secondary schools and universities have led to damaged public property, violence against members of the educational community and clashes with law enforcement.

Mayors of eight Parisian districts had contacted the regional education authority to call for measures to "secure the entrances and exits" of blockaded secondary schools, local media report.

Some 1,548 people have been arrested in Paris since the start of the protests, Faure said on Sunday.

Riot police clash with demonstrators in Paris on Saturday

On Saturday, 106,000 people from a range of groups took to the streets in France, including 24,000 in Paris, the interior ministry said.

It marked the fifth Saturday in a row where students, unions and yellow-vest activists went out to protest.

The number of protesters was much lower than previously, and so was the number of those arrested, the ministry said - 219 across the country, including 45 in Paris.

#20. Two skulls likely belonging to enslaved people returned to Brazil after Harvard used them in eugenics studies

Remains believed to have belonged to Africans or Afro-Brazilians repatriated after being flown back by diplomats

Two skulls, including that of a man believed to have taken part in Brazil’s greatest slave revolt but whose remains were taken to the United States and used in racist eugenics studies at Harvard University, were repatriated on Wednesday.

The remains had been outside Brazil since the 19th century. One was taken about 190 years ago from Salvador, the capital of the state of Bahia; the other, about 160 years ago, from Rio de Janeiro – and were welcomed back in moving, subdued ceremonies in both cities.

The skulls are believed to have belonged to Africans or Afro-Brazilians in the country that received more enslaved Africans than any other during the transatlantic slave trade.

Each was brought back in a padded box inside a reinforced suitcase, carried on flights by Brazilian diplomats after a four-year campaign to recover the remains from Harvard’s Peabody Museum of Archaeology and Ethnology in the US.

Brazilian researchers from local universities will now try to learn more about who the two individuals were.

The skulls will not be displayed in museums or have their images released, and the Brazilian government has pledged that they will be buried with funeral rites as soon as the identification process is complete.

“It’s very important to restore the dignity of these people, which was taken from them in life and in death,” said Carolina Dias dos Reis, a government official who travelled to Boston to sign the agreement with Harvard.

The existence of the skulls was revealed in 2022, and Brazilian researchers sought their repatriation from Harvard, but said they faced a drawn-out process that only moved forward after the Brazilian government became involved in late 2024.

In recent months, concerns grew that Donald Trump’s sometimes hostile behaviour towards his Brazilian counterpart, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, could derail the repatriation. But everything worked out.

“We need to recover our past as a country, however difficult and violent it was, so that we can understand what we are living through in the present: the consequences and scars of structural racism,” added Reis.

Slavery was abolished in 1888, but nearly a century and a half later, Afro-Brazilians make up the majority of the population while occupying the bottom of every socioeconomic indicator, dominating the statistics on hunger, poverty, gender-based violence and victims of homicides committed by criminals or the police.

During the repatriation ceremony in Rio de Janeiro, Ivanir dos Santos, a history professor and a cleric of the Afro-Brazilian religion Candomblé, poured a glass of water on to the ground as researchers received the suitcase.

“It was a ritual act because you cannot deal with the remains of our ancestors without paying reverence to them. This is not a research object … for us, that person is still alive in memory and as our ancestor,” he said.

It is not precisely known who the skulls belonged to. The one taken to Salvador is believed to have belonged to a man who took part in the most significant urban slave revolt in Brazilian history, in 1835, when about 600 African Muslims attempted to seize control of what was then the country’s second most important city. They were ultimately defeated by police, who killed 70 rebels.

The man died at a hospital, and a US diplomat, Gideon T Snow, is believed to have sent the skull to Boston.

The Bahia Islamic Cultural Centre, one of the most active campaigners for its repatriation, has named the man Arakunrin, meaning brother in Yoruba.

Even less is known about the other person: initially, only that they were “an individual … exhumed from the ‘streets of Rio de Janeiro’” in the 1860s. Brazilian government officials, however, said on Wednesday that Harvard researchers had told them that some features – including that the skull was smaller than that of an adult man but larger than that of a child – suggested it may have belonged to a woman.

Bruno Veras, a historian who was part of the group that closely followed the repatriation, cross-referenced diaries, letters and maps of the city and believes the skull may have been taken from a cemetery for newly arrived Africans who either did not survive the long Atlantic crossing or died soon after disembarking.

The person believed to have taken it to the US was Walter Hunnewell, a student from a wealthy Boston family. He was part of an expedition led by the Harvard professor Louis Agassiz, who was in Brazil trying to document physical types to support his racist theories.

“To steal a skull and take it to another country was violent even in the context of racist research,” said Veras.

Researchers in forensic anthropology will now try to confirm the sex of the individuals and other information through measurements of the skulls – the one in Salvador is complete, while the one in Rio is missing its jaw – and microscopic analyses. If necessary, they will seek authorisation to carry out genetic or dating tests.

“This history of slavery cannot be undone,” said Irina Feisthauer Silveira, a diplomat who carried one of the skulls. “But acknowledging the mistakes of the past with transparency is a first step towards building a more equal, just and less violent future.”

#17. Storm Charles causes floods and flight cancellations across southern Europe

Heavy rain and strong winds have also caused disruption across British Columbia, Canada, over the last week

Storm Charles has brought destructive weather to the Iberian peninsula this week, particularly in Portugal and the far south of Spain. Heavy rain and strong south-westerly winds arrived first in the Portuguese island of Madeira, before the system headed towards the mainland on 11 November. A wind gust of 84.5mph (136 km/h) was recorded at Funchal airport, alongside rainfall totals reaching 355mm (13.97in) in 12 hours. In Lisbon, 183 flights were cancelled on Wednesday, about a third of its normal daily traffic, while Madrid Barajas airport had 155 cancellations.

After causing widespread flooding in Spain and Portugal, the system moved on a north-easterly track through southern France and then towards northern Italy. This resulted in significant flooding in parts of Cannes, some of which caused rock falls and led to the closure of roads along the French Riviera. The Italian weather service issued a red rainfall warning, its highest level, for the area extending from Tuscany to Venezia. Further heavy rain is expected to fall in Italy and the Balkans over the next few days, with the latest forecast models indicating an additional 100mm could accumulate widely, while totals close to 200mm are likely in more mountainous areas.

Successive rounds of heavy rain and strong winds have caused flooding, landslides and disruption across British Columbia, Canada through the last week or so.

Between Monday and Friday last week, severe rainfall and strong winds left more than 400,000 customers without power in British Columbia. These unsettled conditions continued into the weekend, with further heavy rain leaving 135,000 customers without power, alongside downed trees, flooded roads and landslides. Strong winds were also recorded on Sunday, with a gust of 72.1mph (116 km/h) on Herbert Island and a gust of 60mph (96 km/h) at Victoria international airport.

Heavy rain warnings were then issued on Wednesday for British Columbia’s Vancouver Island and southern coastal areas as multiple stations recorded rainfall totals in excess of 100mm. Additionally, the River Forecast Centre issued flood warnings on Wednesday across the south coast with heavy rain and high streamflow continuing to bring an increased risk of flash flooding and landslides.

A further area of low pressure is expected to bring torrential rain and strong winds across this same region over the weekend. More than 100mm is likely across central and southern parts of Vancouver Island on Saturday, with gusts in excess of 60mph likely too.

#16. Czechs rally against plan to switch funding of public media

Organisers said around 400,000 people took part on Sunday

Thousands of people have gathered in central Prague to protest against the Czech government's plans to scrap the licence fee and use the state budget to fund public media.

Critics say the plans would allow politicians to stifle media freedom.

The coalition government - which consists of the populist ANO party and two right-wing Eurosceptic parties - insists the change will not affect editorial independence, but wants broadcasters to cut costs.

Parliament passed the first draft of the proposal late last month. Since then, thousands of Czechs have taken part in protests.

Czech households currently pay â 205 crowns ($9.44; Â£7.06) a month for public TV and radio.

Sunday's protest was organised by the Million Moments for Democracy association which said "roughly 400,000" people had attended.

"I know that our entire Czech Television is a guarantee of truth and objectivity," pensioner Milana Rudolfova, 72, told Reuters news agency.

"If we do not have public television and radio then unfortunately we will not be able to believe anything anymore."

The protesters say the planned changes could allow politicians to stifle media freedom

Prime Minister Andrej Babis has insisted the government does not want to control public media.

"The funding system is changing, no one wants to control public media, no one wants to dictate to them what they should say and do, no one wants to nationalise them," Babis told the Czech News Agency.

"We are doing this because the system is already outdated and most of Europe is going the same way today and others are joining in," he added.

Some European countries, including France and Norway, have moved away from the public licence fee model in the last few years.

France removed it in 2022 and its public media, which also carries advertising, is now funded by a portion of the income from VAT.

Norway abolished its license fee in 2020 and moved its public broadcaster's funding into the national budget.

Reuters said the proposal would amount to a 15% cut in funding for the public broadcasters Czech TV and Czech Radio.

These protesters held a sign depicting the animated character Vecernicek and saying "We stand with Czech TV"

Some protesters wore pointed hats folded out of newspapers as a tribute to an animated character, Vecernicek, who has introduced Czech Television's nightly children's bedtime stories for decades.

Critics have compared the government's media bill to changes in Hungary under former Prime Minister Viktor OrbÃ¡n, who tightly controlled state media when he was in power.

Prime Minister Andrej Babis was appointed in December in 2025, and has faced criticism over his coalition's attacks on Czech media.

"Political attacks against private and public media have become one of the hallmarks of Andrej Babis' premiership," said Pavol Szalai, Prague Bureau director of international press freedom organisation Reporters Without Borders, in June.

#15. Choking fires, deadly heat and devastating floods: 2026 in extreme weather

People around the world have contended with unprecedented weather events and their fatal effects caused by the climate catastrophe

After the world’s hottest summer since records began in 1850, the death toll from extreme weather in 2026 is already in the high tens of thousands. That is a conservative estimate and is certain to rise much higher.

As Cop31 continues and scientists warn that climate breakdown may be accelerating, the Guardian looks back at a year of droughts, fires and floods as a reminder that, unless the world takes urgent action to cut emissions, this disastrous year will be one of the best of the rest of our lives.

Extreme heat fuels wildfires in Australia As temperatures jump nearly 20C (68F) above average, Victoria and other southern states register new heat records, including 50C in Port Augusta and the tiny opal mining town of Andamooka. Melbourne hospitals report a 25% increase in heat-related illnesses, while authorities in many areas struggle with wildfires. Scientists say the supercharged climate has made the heatwave five times more likely. Heatwaves already cause more deaths in Australia than all other natural hazards combined.

Mudslides kill 72 in Brazil after record rain The city of Juiz de Fora in Minas Gerais records its wettest ever month, triggering landslides that sweep away hundreds of homes in poor communities on unstable hillsides. Floods also disrupt Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo. More than 10,000 people are displaced.

Devastating floods across much of the world In Ethiopia, at least 131 people are killed in floods and mudslides that bury entire villages. Eighty die in Kenya and 18 in Tanzania. The Arabian peninsula is battered by fierce winds and heavy downpours that flood the streets of the desert kingdom. Hailstones the size of tennis balls fall in Oman and lightning storms leave cities in the UAE inundated with water. Many areas report record rainfall. At least 30 die in Afghanistan as heavy rains trigger landslides. Hong Kong is soaked in twice the usual monthly precipitation. Flood waters in southern Russia submerge homes and leave more than 300,000 without electricity. Huge tracts of farmland are left underwater in Argentina and Chile.

World’s energy imbalance accelerates rapidly The World Meteorological Organization reveals an alarming buildup of energy in Earth’s system. More than 90% is stored in the oceans, where warming has more than doubled over the past two decades compared with the average over the previous 45 years. This makes weather more extreme and threatens health and food supplies. António Guterres, the UN secretary general, says: “The global climate is in a state of emergency. Planet Earth is being pushed beyond its limits. Every key climate indicator is flashing red.” Guterres urges the world to cut fossil fuel emissions as rapidly as possible to minimise future risks. However, CO2 in the atmosphere continues to rise.

Super typhoon tears through Micronesia, Guam and Saipan At peak intensity on 12 April, Sinlaku registers a wind speed of 295km/h (183mph) – roughly the same as a Shinkansen bullet train and the joint highest recorded by a storm in the western Pacific basin so early in the year. It is also the deadliest in decades, killing 12, leaving 43,000 without power, forcing mass evacuation and causing $1.5bn (£1.1bn) in damages. This will remain one of the most powerful storms of 2026, superseded only by Super Typhoon Champi that will strike Taiwan and Japan in October.

Hundreds of millions endure deadly heat in south Asia At least 47 heat-related deaths are reported in southern Asia as temperatures soar above 45C in several Indian and Bangladeshi cities, and hit a peak of 52C in Pakistan’s southern province of Sindh. Scientists warn that the climate crisis is exposing hundreds of millions of people in south Asia to longer, deadlier pre-monsoon heat.

Ocean heat enters uncharted territory Sea surface temperature graphs begin to jag upwards and away from all previous highs on 21 June, starting a trend that will worsen over the rest of the year. This alarms scientists because oceans absorb more than 90% of the excess energy in Earth’s system. If the seas start to release that heat then the land and air will get warmer more quickly. This coincides with the emergence of the latest El Niño, which is confirmed on 2 June.

Arctic and Antarctic swelter in unprecedented warmth Instead of the usual winter snow and ice, scientists at the Esperanza base on the Trinity peninsula of Antarctica are astonished to see rain and dirt outside their windows after an eight-day heatwave that peaked with a record temperature of 15C on 6 June. At the other end of the planet, the Arctic experiences its warmest ever June. Beliy Island, Siberia, registers 28.3C on 25 June, the highest temperature ever recorded that far north in the Eurasian Arctic.

West African deluge takes heavy toll Relentless rains claim almost 100 lives in west Africa from May to June. Record downpours of up to 140mm (5.5in) in a single day are made five times more likely and 23% more intense by the human-caused climate crisis. Floods and mudslides kill 59 in Côte d’Ivoire, 35 in Ghana and five in Togo.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

Fires choke Canada and Europe Almost 1,000 wildfires sweep across Canada, charring 3m hectares (7.4m acres) and sending a haze of smoke as far as New York. On 15 July Toronto registers the world’s worst air quality as a result of the haze. Smoke kills 4,000 people in Canada each year, almost four times as many as at the start of the century. Meanwhile, in Europe, prolonged droughts create tinderbox conditions for wildfires in Greece, Portugal, Italy, Norway, Germany, Spain and France. They burn 686,000 hectares in Europe, twice the average of the past 20 years.

Extreme weather disrupts US 250th birthday festivities and the World Cup Half of the US population is under a heat alert before 4 July independence celebrations, disrupting Donald Trump’s Great American State Fair on the National Mall in Washington and other events. Dozens of heat-related deaths are reported and thousands experience power cuts.

At least nine World Cup games are played in potentially dangerous heat and humidity, while fierce storms delay three matches by more than an hour, including Mexico v England.

Nepal glacier collapse kills at least 1,300 Lhende Khola river valley communities are swept away after a 2 sq km (0.8 sq miles) slab of rock and glacier ice falls 1,400 metres (4,600ft) in the Himalayas, triggering a flood of water and debris. More than 5,000 people are missing and 8,600 need medical attention. Scientists say unusually warm temperatures and long-term permafrost degradation owing to the climate crisis were among the likely causes of the catastrophe.

Europe’s murderous heat forces reactor shutdown The UK and France are among a host of nations that swelter through the warmest summer (June to August) in European history. The heat kills an estimated 50,000 people in cities across the continent. Two-thirds of those excess deaths are attributable to the climate crisis. Many more are likely to have died in rural areas. As droughts spread, Romania is forced to shut down its only nuclear power station because the cooling system could not be replenished by low water levels in the Danube River.

Record heat and deadly storms lash Asia Hundreds are killed and millions evacuated as the world’s most populous continent experiences its hottest September and severe storms. Typhoon Dujuan kills at least 10 people in Japan, with violent winds of 144km/h and 832mm of rain in only 48 hours on the island of Izu Oshima. Three die in a mudslide in eastern China during Typhoon Saudel. At least 102 are killed in India and Nepal amid rain that is nine times the norm in some places. Eight die and half a million are affected by flooding in Bangkok, Thailand.

Governments warned to brace for peak El Niño The UN disaster relief agency urges countries to prepare for an unprecedented “wrecking ball” El Niño that is forecast to peak around the end of the year. UK authorities advise households to stockpile a few days of basic supplies. Climate scientists project more than 450,000 people globally will die by February as a result of the additional heat brought by the super El Niño.

Protests in Indonesia after deadly fires Demonstrators in Jakarta protest against Indonesia’s worst fire season in a decade, which has scorched almost 2m hectares of forest – more than twice as big an area as last year. The choking haze is estimated to have killed 3,560 people in one month alone.

Roads turn to rivers in Chile After the heaviest October rainfall in 76 years, the Chilean government declares the Santiago district of Las Condes a “disaster zone”, with more than 1,000 people evacuated from their homes, some by helicopter.

Drought and fire in the Amazon rainforest Months of drought leave some of the world’s mightiest rivers, including the Rio Negro, Madeira and Tapajós, close to record low levels, curtailing the water transport of essential goods. This coincides with a sharp rise in fire activity in the central and eastern Amazon region, releasing so much smoke that the city of Manaus registers the second-worst air quality in the world on 12 October.

2026 is forecast to be one of the hottest years in history

Global temperatures tie an all-time record of 2.04C above preindustrial levels, which was set in 2023. This year is forecast to be one of the four warmest years in history, along with 2023, 2024 and 2025. Sea surface temperatures are so high that the climate scientist Eliot Jacobson calculates the odds of being this far above the norm at 33tn to one. Meanwhile, the amount of CO2 in the atmosphere reaches a peak not seen in 2m years. On the plus side, global fossil fuel emissions are likely to fall this year thanks to a shift away from oil and gas owing to high prices after the US-Israeli attack on Iran, and ever cheaper renewable alternatives.

#13. Suspected China spy linked to Nvidia's ex-boss left in 2025, Megaspeed says

The Nvidia scandal has sent shockwaves through Silicon Valley as well as the political establishment

Alice Huang, a suspected China spy who has been linked to Nvidia's former boss Jensen Huang, left her executive role at Megaspeed in August last year, the company says.

Jensen Huang resigned as chief executive of the AI chip-making giant Nvidia after it emerged he had allegedly had affairs with Alice Huang as well as with one of his employees.

Megaspeed is the biggest buyer of Nvidia chips in South East Asia and the scandal at Nvidia has raised questions about the possibility that high-end AI chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

The Singapore-based company said on Sunday it had launched an internal investigation into its business.

Megaspeed said it was aware of reports "containing assertions about our business, supply chain and global operations that are not supported by verifiable evidence".

It added that it "conducts legitimate and lawful business" and has "retained external legal counsel to conduct a thorough internal investigation".

Also on Sunday, US Congressman John Moolenaar said that Jensen Huang has agreed to testify before the Select Committee on China, which he chairs, as well as the House Foreign Affairs and the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence.

The committee heads had threatened to issue subpoenas to Huang if he did not comply with their requests to testify.

Moolenaar told CBS, BBC's US partner, that none were ultimately issued. Huang's testimony is expected to take place behind close doors, with the dates still being finalised.

"That's going to be a very important meeting to understand to what degree privileged information was accessed, and to what degree this potential alleged Chinese spy influenced Nvidia's philosophy and may have had an influence on some of our policies with respect to AI and export controls," the congressman told the Face The Nation programme.

Separately, a group of tech leaders, including Space X's Elon Musk, have been trying to rally support to have Huang reinstated as Nvidia CEO.

A representative for Huang declined to comment on a report in the Financial Times Sunday suggesting that he was discussing a comeback to the firm he co-founded.

On Friday, US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent blamed fellow Trump cabinet colleague Howard Lutnick's commerce department for failing to properly oversee the export of high-end chips.

Bessent told CNBC that several agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months... but Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process".

The so-called entity list restricts US trade with overseas firms and individuals.

Lutnick has been called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over his department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

Other congressional committees have asked both Lutnick and his department to retain all records relating to Nvidia and Jensen Huang.

Megaspeed is suspected of giving backdoor access to Nvidia chips to Chinese companies via its data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Nvidia has been banned from selling its advanced chips to China.

While Singapore does not prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Singapore has said that while it does not enforce "unilateral export controls imposed by other countries" it expects all companies in its jurisdiction to respect such regulations.

Megaspeed said it "designs and maintains its global operational and supply-chain control frameworks to comply with all applicable laws and regulatory regimes". It said this includes "US export control requirements and local jurisdiction rules in every market where we operate".

China has denied that Alice Huang is a spy and warned the US not to use the scandal as a "pretext for imposing a technological blockade" on AI technology.

#11. Kenyan doomsday sect leader jailed for 200 years for murder of 190 children

Paul Mackenzie convicted over deaths of children and one adult after followers were ordered to starve themselves and their families

The leader of a Kenyan doomsday sect has been sentenced to 200 years in prison for the murders of 190 children and one adult in one of the world’s worst cult disasters in recent history.

The sentence, comprising 100 years for murder by starvation and 100 years by direct homicide, came two weeks after the court in Kenya’s coastal city of Mombasa found the cult leader guilty of the crimes.

Prosecutors said Paul Mackenzie and his Good News International church ran ​a Christian doomsday sect whose members were ordered to starve themselves and their children to death to go to heaven before the world ended.

His accomplices threatened and beat victims to ensure they followed his orders. The emaciated bodies were found scattered in shallow graves and dumped in latrines. Some had been buried alive.

Mackenzie was arrested in 2023 after police received a tipoff, and weeks later authorities began uncovering bodies in shallow graves in the Shakahola forest near the coast in south-eastern Kenya.

In total, more than 400 bodies have been recovered. Many were severely decomposed and only 26 could be identified.

Mackenzie denied murder charges, saying he did not force the victims to starve, but the court rejected that argument.

The Shakahola tragedy has gripped Kenya since the discovery of mass graves in the forest more than three years ago.

It raised questions about how Mackenzie was able to continue operating despite being on the radar of the authorities since 2017, when police searched his premises.

In 2019, authorities shut down Mackenzie’s church and he relocated ‌to the Shakahola forest, police said.

The case has also led to calls for tougher oversight of churches and other religious organisations in a country where religious faith plays a prominent role in public life.

Mackenzie, a former taxi driver, forbade cult members from sending their children to school and from going to hospital when they were ill, describing such institutions as satanic.

Cult members lived in makeshift homes of polythene sheeting and thatch that Mackenzie divided into areas with biblical names such as Jerusalem and Judea.

The plight of his followers began ​to emerge in March 2023, when a man told ​police his brother and wife had starved their children ​to death in the forest on Mackenzie’s orders.

Mackenzie was arrested but released on bail, after which relatives said he brought forward the date ​on which he told his followers the ‌world would end. Police rearrested him and ​started combing through the forest more systematically and began exhuming mass graves.

Last month’s judgment covered 191 deaths while investigations into more than 200 other deaths are continuing.

Justice Diana Kavedza-Mochache said in a hearing lasting more than eight hours that in some cases children were isolated and denied food while their parents watched.

Mackenzie faces other charges over the forest deaths, including those of terrorism, which he has denied.

Prosecutors said that while he was in detention he also directed the deaths of 52 people in Kwa Binzaro village, about 18 miles from Shakahola. Mackenzie has pleaded not guilty in that case.

#10. Israel Kidnapped Iranian Generals in Hunt for Ron Arad

Israeli officials were convinced that Tehran was to blame for the airman’s disappearance. Alternative theories got little attention, until now.

Israeli officials were convinced that Tehran was to blame for the airman’s disappearance. Alternative theories got little attention, until now.

At a protest in 2025 in Tel Aviv, supporters of Israeli hostages held captive by Hamas held pictures of the hostages and of Ron Arad, who has been missing since 1986.Credit...Itai Ron/Reuters

By Adam GoldmanRonen BergmanFarnaz Fassihi and Hwaida Saad

Ronen Bergman, Farnaz Fassihi, Adam Goldman and Hwaida Saad have spent years investigating the disappearance of the Israeli airman Ron Arad.

Gen. Abdullah Saberi, an Iranian commander, stepped out for a jog early one morning in an upscale diplomatic quarter of Syria’s capital. Israeli intelligence officers waited nearby. Posing as movers, they had slipped past the neighborhood’s security checkpoints.

As he headed for a nearby park, General Saberi passed two men who carried an oversize refrigerator box. In a flash, the men restrained the general, drugged him, threw the box over his head, shoved him into a white van and left as if nothing had happened.

He was whisked to Israel for interrogation, according to numerous officials in the Middle East, including senior Iranian officials close to General Saberi and a Syrian official who reviewed video of the September 2021 kidnapping.

As a veteran of Iran’s powerful Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps, General Saberi knew many secrets. But his interrogators were interested in only one.

Mr. Arad, an Israeli airman, vanished decades ago, and the Israeli military says he is the only service member classified as missing in action. The government’s long search for him has captivated the public and has come to symbolize Israel’s commitment, rooted in Jewish tradition, to bringing its soldiers home, dead or alive. Articles, books and documentaries have been produced about the hunt for Mr. Arad.

The Israeli government was so determined to find him that, even four decades after his disappearance, it took the risk of kidnapping General Saberi in the shadow of his own embassy.

#9. How African philosophies inspired a new approach to staging film festivals in Manchester

See My World, an event highlighting the pan-African thought via film, poetry and dance, builds on momentum sparked by local activists and creatives

African philosophies including exploring how lessons from the past can shape the present and near future have inspired a film festival coming to Manchester.

See My World, a day dedicated to independent creators from across the African diaspora in the north of England, will take place at the Contact theatre on 21 November.

The festival is part of See My World’s year-round programme showcasing pan-African arts, culture and events.

It reflects the determination among creatives and activists in Manchester to build on the legacy of the 1945 Pan-African Congress in Manchester – which connected luminaries of postwar independence movements with a shared vision of liberation – after last year’s 80th anniversary commemorations.

See My World’s CEO and artistic director, Tunde Adekoya, said this month’s film festival was taking inspiration from across the spectrum of pan-African thought, including the works of Kenyan writers John Mbiti, an Anglican theologian who died in 2019, and Ngũgĩ wa Thiong’o, the author of the groundbreaking 1986 book Decolonising the Mind who died last year.

Both writers rejected the colonial conceit that African thought was inferior, stressed the importance of community and saw reclaiming African ways of knowing as essential to a people’s self-understanding.

While Mbiti sought common ground between African religions and Christianity, Ngũgĩ was a Marxist. He argued colonialism was motivated by cultural and economic conquest, and that European powers used language as their sharpest instrument. Ngũgĩ famously abandoned English to write in Gikuyu, a Bantu language mainly spoken in Kenya and criticised the role missionary Christianity played in colonial rule.

Adekoya said: “It’s a day dedicated to pan-African and global majority films from the north of England with exclusive premieres of documentaries we’ve been working on, which weave together expressions of liberation through art forms like poetry and dance.

Starting with the Guardian’s own history, Guardian journalists explore the legacies of enslavement and reparative justice around the world

“The whole idea is that it’s like a playground in search of strategies of liberation, using the festival as a way to explore different philosophies.

“John Mbiti explores the philosophy and spiritual understandings of time through the concept of Sasa na Zamani – in which the past informs everything that happens moving forward. That reflects how we constantly go back to pan-African histories to inform how we progress.”

Film-makers have been invited to submit films lasting up to 30 minutes from across genres, with no screening fees charged.

As well as submissions, the festival will feature documentaries produced by See My World. They include Homage, a “cinematic love letter to dance” that traces a culture through present-day portraits illustrating dance artists and archival material from Manchester, telling a story of joy, loss, migration, rhythm and resistance. Meanwhile, Poetic Justice explores how pan-African thought and Manchester’s poetry scene speak across decades.

Adekoya added: “The idea is that we would go against the norm of how film festivals are, because when we think about decolonisation, we think about how structures have been created to alienate and exclude communities.

“So we thought our film festival would look more at how we make film accessible and how stories that deserve to be seen get seen. Throughout the day we will have different activities, from exhibitions to explorations of our performances, discussions, Q&As, insights into directors’ processes, and workshops.

“We’ve been looking for literature and concepts that inform us and give us an understanding of who we truly are. And as works from thinkers like John Mbiti have come into our sphere, they have really emboldened us to think differently about our place in the world and what we do.”

See My World film festival runs from 2pm to 11pm on Saturday 21 November at Contact theatre, Oxford Road, Manchester.

#8. Pope Leo rides in helicopter cockpit during Peru trip

Pope Leo XIV shared a cockpit with pilots from the Peruvian Air Force during his tour of Latin America.

On Sunday, the pontiff arrived in Cusco, a southern Andes Mountains city that was once the heart of the Inca empire.

The Peruvian leg of his tour holds special significance for the Pope.

He not only lived in Peru for long periods between 1985 and 2023 but also became a citizen of the country in 2015, forging close ties with local communities.

#6. South Korea's top scorer in tax accountancy exam admits cheating

Han Won-seok confessed in a video on the YouTube channel of his lawyer

A candidate who came first in South Korea's certified tax accountancy exam has admitted cheating by using a miniature camera, mobile phone and AI tools.

Han Won-seok confessed in a video on the YouTube account of his lawyer, Lee Don-Ho, adding that he had confessed to the Seoul Metropolitan Police Agency and offered details on how he cheated.

The 34-year-old scored 397.5 out of 400 points in the first round, getting just one of 160 questions wrong, which initially raised suspicions and sparked online speculation.

Standardised national tests play an outsized role in career trajectories for South Koreans, making their results - and efforts to unduly inflate those results - of great importance.

"I cheated in both the first and second rounds of the tax account exam by using electronic devices and generative AI," Han said in the video published on Sunday.

"I used a microcamera to photograph the test questions and sent them to an AI then checked my answers on my smartphone to commit the cheating."

When accusations he had cheated first began to surface, Han had denied any wrongdoing - but said he had changed his mind after thinking about the feelings of the candidate who had come second in the exam.

He went on to apologise to the other entrants who had passed the tests fairly.

"It seems that because of me, other successful candidates are being suspected," he suggested.

The Ministry of Employment and Labour and the Human Resources Development Service held an emergency meeting after Han admitted cheating.

The South Korean president's policy chief Ha Jun-kyung said new measures would be introduced to prevent cheating and warned "past crimes" would be "tracked down and apprehended to the very end".

Those found to have used AI to cheat in the tax accountancy exam will have their qualification revoked and will be banned from retaking the test for five years, the government said.

The scandal comes just days before the east Asian nation's annual college entrance exam, a crucial hurdle for students.

Can smart glasses exam cheating be stopped?

A series of cases involving exam cheating with AI glasses in South Korea has universities and schools scrambling to rein in the use of the technology.

Han said he had only cheated to try and earn money and get a job that would make his parents proud of him again.

Seoul Metropolitan Police Agency told Korean news agency Yonhap on Sunday that it was looking into the case.

"We received a written confession through the test taker's lawyer this morning regarding cheating on the tax accountant exam," it confirmed in a statement.

It added that the case had now been assigned to investigators.

Because of the significance of the national exams, instances of cheating are not uncommon in South Korea, and cases involving the use of AI have become more prominent as the technology becomes more sophisticated.

Last month, two college applicants were reported to have used AI glasses to try and cheat their entrance exams at two Seoul universities.

Education ministry data also says there were four cases of cheating through AI glasses at middle and high schools in the past 12 months.

Exams watchdog warns of rise in high-tech cheating

#5. Collision of two trains in Egypt kills at least seven

President orders prompt investigation after one train derails in collision in al-Ayyat about 50km from Cairo

Seven people were killed and at least 125 injured when two trains collided on Sunday south of the Egyptian capital, Cairo, officials said.

The crash occurred in al-Ayyat district in Giza province, about 50km (30 miles) south of Cairo, when a train travelling to Fayoum province crashed into the rear of a train heading to the southern province of Aswan, according to the transport ministry.

An inspection indicated that the Cairo-Aswan train had been stationary on the same track, officials said.

The health ministry gave the casualty toll, adding more than 70 of the injured were discharged after receiving treatment.

Train derailments and crashes are common in Egypt, where the railway system has a history of mismanagement and poor maintenance of equipment. In recent years the government has announced modernisation initiatives to improve its railways.

Ayman Gamal, a resident of Kafr Girza in al-Ayyat where the crash occurred, said people rushed to the scene, pulled out survivors and called ambulances.

Gamal said he saw about 80 of the injured being taken by ambulances to hospital. Some passengers were trapped inside one of the carriages of the stationary train and died, he said.

Egypt’s president, Abdel Fatah al-Sisi, said in a social media post that he had ordered a prompt investigation.

Outside al-Wasta central hospital in Beni Suef province, where some of the injured were taken for treatment, survivors said passengers were in shock and struggling to breathe because of all the dust that was kicked up. Nearby villagers eventually got them out, they said.

Farha Gamal, a survivor who was inside the Fayyoum train, said she saw some of the passengers who appeared dead and others were struggling to find their way out of the car. “There were other cars that were completely mangled,” she said in an interview.

Emergency teams and cranes were dispatched to the crash site to clear the wreckage and restore train services to their normal schedule as soon as possible, the Egyptian railway authority said.

Last September, a train driver and his assistant were injured after two freight trains collided in the same district.

Egypt’s deadliest train crash was in 2002 when more than 300 people were killed after a fire broke out on an overnight train journeying from Cairo to southern Egypt.

#4. Lewis Hamilton visits Egypt’s Rafah crossing and Gaza aid facilities

Seven-time F1 world champion tours border area and logistics warehouses operated by Egyptian Red Crescent

Lewis Hamilton has visited Egypt’s Rafah border crossing with the Gaza Strip to meet organisations sending humanitarian aid to the territory.

The seven-time Formula One world champion has spoken out about conflict and humanitarian crises in the region, and has called for peace. He has frequently posted on social media about children killed in Israeli airstrikes in Gaza and called for a ceasefire.

“I think we need to make sure we’re pushing to free Congo, free Sudan, free Syria, free Palestine. I think there’s a lot of peace that I’m hoping we get to at some stage,” Hamilton said in October.

A ceasefire agreement reached last year has halted major combat operations in Gaza, but the amount of aid reaching the territory continues to fall far short of the agreed-upon minimum of 600 trucks a day.

Israeli forces have continued to advance, expanding their control from 53% of the territory to at least 60%, an independent commission of UN experts said last month.

Airstrikes also continue, and Israeli fire along the advancing front has killed at least 196 Palestinians, including 18 women, 32 boys and 11 girls, the commission said.

Hamilton toured Egypt’s Rafah border crossing with Gaza before visiting logistics warehouses operated by the Egyptian Red Crescent, which facilitates the entry of aid to Gaza.

At the crossing, Hamilton greeted aid workers with “Assalamu alaikum” a widely used Arabic greeting meaning “peace be upon you.” He shook hands and briefly spoke to workers and officials.

Khaled Megawer, the governor of North Sinai, where Rafah is located, said the visit was intended to help Hamilton learn about Egypt’s efforts to send aid to Gaza.

The visit comes after Hamilton made his 400th race start on Sunday at the São Paulo Grand Prix at Interlagos.

The first black F1 driver, he made his debut with McLaren in 2007. He is only the second F1 driver to start 400 races after his former teammate Fernando Alonso, who achieved the milestone in 2024.

#3. Zelensky urges US and Musk to lift Starlink restrictions and be on 'right side of history'

Restrictions have affected Ukraine's ability to intercept Russian missiles and drones

A crucial US satellite internet service is still operating too slowly and affecting Ukraine's ability to stop Russian attacks, President Volodymyr Zelensky has said.

"We are counting on these restrictions being lifted. The protection of lives and Ukraine's energy infrastructure ahead of winter directly depends on this," he said.

The issue over the speed of the Starshield system - a military-grade Starlink service owned by Elon Musk and provided to Ukraine by the US government - has been rumbling on for weeks.

Zelensky said he had asked officials "to work as closely as possible" with the US government and Musk's company. "It is important to be on the right side of history," he said. The BBC has asked the White House and Starlink for comment.

US has floated Ukraine ceding Donbas for Nato membership, Zelensky says

Ukraine uses the normal Starlink service for communications on the battlefield, while Starshield is used for the interception of missiles and drones in Ukraine airspace.

The issue of speed restrictions was first identified in October. It led to conflicting accounts by both Elon Musk and US President Donald Trump.

Musk said the US government was responsible as Starshield was controlled by them, but Trump denied being responsible and said he had told the billionaire owner to "put it back".

It appeared to be returning to normal after a week, following which Ukraine said the restrictions were still being implemented.

The connection issues coincided with a tense diplomatic stand-off between Zelensky and Trump over Ukrainian strikes on oil infrastructure within Russia.

Trump said the strikes on Russian oil refineries, which Kyiv says are crucial to stemming the main revenue source for Moscow's war effort, had pushed global prices higher.

On Sunday Zelensky said Ukraine was struggling to intercept Russian jet-powered drones targeting electricity substations because "restrictions on the operating speed of Starshield terminals along the front line and in the temporarily occupied territories are still there".

"Our interceptor drones lose connection with their operators at a speed of 161km/h (100mph) and simply cannot catch up with Russian jet-powered 'shaheds'," he posted, referring to the drones that follow an Iranian design.

On Friday Zelensky said speed limits of around 100mph had been enforced on the network since Monday, but only in Russian-occupied areas and not the whole of Ukraine.

"It already has political implications. If you impose restrictions there, you are already partially recognising the temporary occupied territories as not ours," the New York Times reported him as saying.

Ukraine has also been beset by a shortage of US-made Patriot interceptors used to defend against Russian missiles - a situation that Russia has exploited to intensify strikes on Ukrainian targets.

Overnight and into Sunday morning Russia hit energy infrastructure in Zaporizhzhia and the Sumy, Odesa, Zhytomyr, and Kirovohrad regions, Ukraine said.

It also struck railway infrastructure in the Dnipro, Kharkiv, and Zaporizhzhia regions.

Scores of people are said to have been hurt in those attacks, as well as two in the Donetsk and Kyiv regions.

Two people were killed in an attack on an infrastructure facility in Chernihiv Region's Mena, governor Vyacheslav Chaus said.

Multiple strikes were also delivered on energy facilities across the region, he added on Telegram.

Ukraine meanwhile said it had carried out strikes on a petrochemical plant and an oil refinery in the central Russian city of Nizhnekamsk.

"On the night of November 15, units of Ukraine's Defence Forces reached Nizhnekamsk in Tatarstan," the military said on Telegram.

"Two enterprises that feed the Russian war with raw materials and fuel came under attack."

Other Ukrainian strikes killed one person in Taganrog, an industrial port city near the border, regional governor Yuri Slyusar said.

In the occupied Luhansk region a lorry driver and an elderly woman were also killed, Leonid Pasechnik, the regional leader installed by Moscow said.

Meanwhile Estonia has announced it will shut its border crossing points with Russia from 1 December following a suspected sabotage attack on a defence company.

Eight people were detained over an attempted arson attack on drone maker Threod Systems.

The Estonian authorities say they had been ordered by Russia's GRU military intelligence service to try and carry out the attack. Russia has not commented.

"Russia must understand that hostile actions against Estonia come with consequences," Foreign Minister Margus Tsahkna said.
